# Commendation

->Prof. D. Prahladacharya<-
->Vice-Chancellor<-
->National Sanskrit University<-
->Tirupati<-

Recognizing that ignorance is the primary cause of the various types of suffering endured by humanity, the ancient great seers <note>Rishis</note>, who were dedicated to the welfare of the entire world and committed to removing that ignorance, revealed many systems of philosophy <note>Darshanas</note> through the power of their great penance. Among these, the system of Astrology <note>Jyotisha-darshana</note> holds a significant place. This is indeed one of the "limbs of the Vedas" <note>Vedangas; auxiliary sciences required for the understanding and application of Vedic knowledge</note>. 

This science is not only helpful to the Vedic scholars <note>Shrotriyas</note> who are intent on performing Vedic rituals—by determining the appropriate times for such ceremonies—but it is also extremely beneficial to every common person. It assists in achieving success in both worldly and spiritual actions by providing knowledge of the suitable time <note>Muhurta</note> and other factors. This fact is accepted by many discerning people, even those influenced by the impact of modern physical sciences.

It is well known to scholars familiar with this science that there are many important manuscripts related to this "Eye of the Veda" <note>Veda-chakshus; a traditional metaphor for Astrology as it allows one to 'see' the proper time for action</note>—which consists of three branches: Mathematical Astronomy <note>Siddhanta</note>, Omens/General Astrology <note>Samhita</note>, and Natal Astrology <note>Hora</note>—waiting to be published in various places. Among such works is the text named *Muhūrtaratna* <note>The Jewel of Electional Astrology</note>. This was composed by the eminent scholar Govinda Bhattiri, who was born in Kerala in the 13th century CE and whose name is held in high esteem. Although the title suggests the work deals only with the consideration of auspicious moments <note>Muhurta</note>, the uniqueness of this book lies in the fact that it also presents other topics related to the science of light <note>Jyotish-shastra</note>. With its extremely useful...

# Introduction
<note>original: "Upodghātaḥ"</note>

In this world, all living beings desire only happiness. For that very reason, they engage and excel in the methods of securing their own food and recreation. Although activities such as eating and recreation are common to all creatures, human beings—who are distinct from other animals due to their faculty of discernment <note>original: "viveka"</note> and who consider material objects to be illusory—desire only that unsurpassed, supreme happiness. This desire is expressed as: "May happiness be mine; may suffering never befall me." This is synonymous with natural will. It is a universal truth that all activities arise for this purpose, and toward this end, everyone strives.

Furthermore, when the inquiry arises as to how that unsurpassed happiness is achieved, the established solution is: only through the knowledge and practice of the meaning of the Vedas. The Veda is indeed our greatest wealth. The Veda has been defined by the Commentators <note>referring to traditional scholars like Sāyaṇa or Śaṅkara</note> as "a collection of words not of human origin" <note>original: "apauruṣeyaṃ vākyam"</note> and "that which makes known the supernatural means of attaining the desired and avoiding the undesired." Based on the etymology "that by which the goals of human life <term>Puruṣārthas: the four aims of life—Duty, Wealth, Pleasure, and Liberation</term> such as Righteousness are known and obtained," it is certain that without Vedic knowledge, Righteousness and the other goals cannot be realized. If Righteousness, Wealth, and Pleasure are not realized, then the fourth goal—Liberation <note>original: "Mokṣa"</note>, which is the nature of unsurpassed happiness—will certainly not be realized either. Therefore, those who solely desire to attain unsurpassed happiness must properly practice Righteousness, Wealth, and Pleasure.

## The Limbs of the Veda
<note>original: "Vedāṅgāni"</note>

Now, out of a desire to lift everyone across the ocean of this delusive worldly existence, the most compassionate Great Sages revealed and composed the "Limbs of the Veda" <note>the six auxiliary sciences: phonetics, ritual, grammar, etymology, meter, and astronomy</note> and the Philosophical Systems <note>original: "Darśanas"</note> to facilitate the understanding of the Veda's meaning; this is indeed the great fortune of the people. Understanding the meaning of the Veda is not possible without knowledge of the Limbs of the Veda. This being the case, it is no exaggeration to say that knowledge of these Limbs is mandatory. Therefore, for the performance of rituals, the Limbs of the Veda must also be understood.

The supreme and ultimate goal of the Veda, its Limbs, and the Philosophical Systems is Liberation alone. There is no dispute that nothing is achieved without the performance of action <note>original: "karma"</note>. The steps toward that goal are as follows: from the performance of action comes the destruction of sins; from the destruction of sins comes the dawn of the knowledge of reality; from the knowledge of reality comes the destruction of false knowledge; from the destruction of false knowledge comes the destruction of attachment and aversion; from the destruction of attachment comes the destruction of delusion; and from the destruction of delusion comes Liberation. Thus it is said: "One destroys sins only by performing the obligatory and occasional rituals." Therefore, the performance of obligatory and occasional rituals is an absolute necessity. For that purpose, [the study] of the Limbs of the Veda...

<summary>This introductory text argues that ultimate happiness (Liberation) can only be achieved through Vedic knowledge and the performance of rituals, which require the study of the six auxiliary sciences known as the Vedangas.</summary>
<keywords>Vedas, Vedangas, Moksha, Purusharthas, Dharma, Karma, Liberation, Sanskrit philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page serves as the introductory justification for the study of Astrology (Jyotisha) within the Vedic tradition, establishing its role as the "eye" of the Vedas and the essential tool for timing rituals.</meta>

<page-num>ii</page-num>
<header>The Gem of Electional Astrology</header>

Knowledge is absolutely necessary. These are the limbs of the Vedas <note>The Vedangas: six auxiliary disciplines traditionally studied to understand and apply the Vedas</note> —

> 'The meters are the feet of the Veda, and the ritual instructions are called its hands.
> Astrology is the eye, and etymology is called the ear.
>
> 'Phonetics is the nose of the Veda, and grammar is remembered as its mouth.
> Therefore, only by studying the Vedas along with these limbs is one honored in the world of the Creator.'

### The Importance of Astrology Among the Vedic Limbs

This science of Astrology, being the eye of the Veda, enjoys the highest importance and primacy among the six limbs. This is because Astrology is the science that determines time. "Determining time" means it is the communicator of time. Ritual activities such as sacrifices and offerings, when performed in accordance with a <term>Muhurta</term> <note>An auspicious period of time or "election" chosen for starting an activity</note>, lead to the desired results, and not when performed at random. Thus, it has been said by the learned:

> The Vedas are indeed directed toward the purpose of sacrifice,
> and sacrifices are prescribed according to the sequence of time.
> Therefore, he who knows this science of time-keeping,
> which is Astrology, knows the sacrifices.

For instance, there are Vedic injunctions such as: "One should offer oblations at sunrise," "One should offer oblations before sunrise," "One should bathe during an eclipse," or "One should sacrifice at the new and full moon." But whose sunrise? Whose eclipse? What is this "new moon"? In the absence of such knowledge of time, when one asks how these duties should be performed, Astrology alone provides the answer. The <term>Daivajnas</term> <note>Astrologers; literally "knowers of the divine/fate"</note> determine the time of sunrise, non-sunrise, the new moon, and eclipses through minute observation, tracking the movements of the sun and other celestial bodies with extreme precision. Only through their constant labor is the time produced that is fit and praised for the performance of sacrifices and rituals.

It was stated earlier that Astrology is the determiner of time. However, it is not only the determiner of time, but also the decider of the past, present, and future. Following the noble saying, "We are the sharers of the fruits of our actions," people in this world wonder and desire to know: what will be the result of the work already done, the work being done, and the work yet to be done? There is no means in this world to answer this other than Astrology.

<vocab>Limb of the Veda, Astrology, Science of time-keeping, Sacrifice, Astrologer, Auspicious moment, Time-determiner, Meter, Grammar</vocab>

<page-num>iii</page-num>
->Introduction<-

## The Sub-divisions of Astrology

For each of the four Vedas, a distinct branch of astronomical science is found: the Rig-Jyotisha, the Yajur-Jyotisha, the Sama-Jyotisha, and the Atharva-Jyotisha. Over the course of time, this science underwent significant development. As time passed, this discipline was divided into three main branches <note>literally "trunks" or "skandhas"</note>: Mathematical Astronomy (*Siddhanta*), Omens and Mundane Astrology (*Samhita*), and Natal Astrology (*Hora*). As stated by the sage Narada:

> The supreme science of astrology is the pure eye of the Veda, 
> consisting of three branches: Siddhanta, Samhita, and Hora.

Furthermore, in the text *Prashna Marga* <note>A famous 17th-century South Indian treatise on horary astrology</note>:

> The great teachers declare that the vast science of astrology possesses six limbs: 
> Natal Astrology (*Jataka*), Observational Astronomy (*Gola*), Omens (*Nimitta*), 
> Inquiry (*Prashna*), Electional Astrology (*Muhurta*), and Mathematics (*Ganita*).

Thus, scholars recognize these six limbs—Natal Astrology and the others—within the discipline. This science has attained an even more expansive form, becoming enriched by fields such as psychology and medicine. However, upon close examination, all these considerations are ultimately contained within the original three branches.

## Natal Astrology (Hora)

Scholars believe that the word *Hora* is derived from the word *Ahoratra* <note>meaning "day and night," or a full 24-hour cycle</note>. As stated in the *Brihat Jataka* <note>The foundational text of Indian predictive astrology by Varahamihira</note>:

> Some maintain that the word 'Hora' is formed by dropping the first and last syllables 
> of 'Ahoratra' <note>original: "aho-ratra" becomes "ho-ra"</note>. 
> It reveals the ripening of the good and evil fruits of actions 
> earned by an individual in their previous lives.

This branch of *Hora* is also known by the name *Jataka Skandha* (the branch of birth). In this section, the auspicious and inauspicious results that will occur in a person's life are predicted according to the positions of the planets at the time of birth. Within this branch of *Hora*, many topics are examined, such as: the nature of planets and zodiac signs, the consideration of colors, directional strengths, the twelve houses, the mutual aspects of planets, friendships and enmities, the system of eight-fold points (*Ashtakavarga*), the determination of longevity, combinations for royal success (*Raja Yogas*), and the major and minor planetary periods (*Dashas* and *Antardashas*).

<summary>This introductory page outlines the historical evolution of Indian astrology from its Vedic roots into three primary branches and six specialized limbs, focusing specifically on the etymology and scope of Natal Astrology (Hora).</summary>
<keywords>Vedic astrology, Siddhanta, Samhita, Hora, Narada, Prashna Marga, Brihat Jataka, karma, planetary periods, zodiac signs</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory overview of the branches of Jyotisha (Vedic astrology and astronomy), specifically focusing on the Horā (predictive) and Siddhānta (mathematical/theoretical) sections.</meta>

<page-num>iv</page-num>
->The Jewel of Electional Astrology<-

*The Great Birth*, *The Collection of Essence*, *The Method of Natal Astrology*, *The Jewel of Natal Astrology*, and *The Rosary of Natal Astrology* are some of the famous <term>Horā texts</term> <note>Horā refers to predictive astrology focusing on individual birth charts</note>. Among natal texts, the **"Great Birth"** (Brihat Jataka) by the teacher Varahamihira holds a position of great importance.

## Siddhānta (Mathematical Astronomy)

In this branch of Siddhānta, the positions of the planets and stars in the sky, the specific movements of the planets, planetary orbits, the <term>method of calculating elapsed days</term> <note>original: ahargana-anayana-krama; the calculation of the number of days passed since a fixed epoch</note>, the <term>procedure for clarifying planetary positions</term> <note>original: graha-spashtikarana; calculating the true longitude of planets from their mean positions</note>, and the consideration of solar and lunar eclipses are described. Similarly, the determination of direction, place, and time, the <term>nine different systems of time measurement</term> <note>such as Solar, Civil (Savana), Lunar, etc.</note>, <term>trigonometric calculations</term> <note>specifically the calculation of sines (jya)</note>, and <term>corrections for terrestrial latitude and longitude</term> are also subjects of study in this branch.

A text where the <term>clarification of planets</term> is performed by calculating the <term>elapsed days</term> from the beginning of the Kalpa <note>a vast cosmic cycle of 4.32 billion years</note> is called a <term>Siddhānta</term>. Where the elapsed days are brought from the beginning of a Yuga <note>an age or epoch</note>, it is called a Tantra. And where the elapsed days and planetary operations are calculated starting from a chosen epoch year, that is called a <term>Karana</term> <note>a practical astronomical handbook</note>. Furthermore, it is divided into three parts: Arithmetic, Algebra, and Geometry. In very ancient times, Indian mathematicians conducted research with great effort in the field of mathematics and established many principles. The <term>decimal system</term> discovered by ancient Indian astronomers is accepted even by modern scholars. The principle of the Earth's force of attraction (gravity), which has been presented by modern scientists, is seen clearly established by Bhaskaracharya in the text **The Crest-Jewel of Siddhāntas**. As it is said:

> This Earth possesses an attractive power; by that power, she pulls any heavy object in the sky toward herself. That object appears to be falling, but in space, where could it fall? It is being pulled from all sides equally.

Aryabhata's **Aryabhatiya**, Lalla's **Increaser of Intelligence**, Brahmagupta's **The Correct Siddhānta of Brahma**, and Bhaskaracharya's **The Crest-Jewel of Siddhāntas** are famous Siddhānta texts.

## Samhitā (Mundane Astrology)

In this branch of Samhitā, the auspicious and inauspicious results concerning the nation are determined, rather than those of an individual. The good and bad effects on the world caused by the movement of the Sun and other planets through the circle of constellations...

# Introduction

In this section <note>literally "skandha" or branch of the astrological tree</note>, various topics are examined, such as the rising of the Seven Sages <note>the stars of the Big Dipper</note> led by Agastya <note>the star Canopus</note>, the conjunctions and conflicts of the planets, the yearly forecasts, and the planetary configurations known as "Shrungataka." Furthermore, it describes the signs of cloud formation <note>conception of rain</note>, the retention of the clouds, the timing of rainfall, immediate rain, and the signs indicated by the flowering and fruiting of plants. Additionally, the effects of omens such as the "burning of the directions" <note>atmospheric glows</note>, earthquakes, the appearance of comets, the falling of meteors, and the appearance of mock suns are discussed.

The chapters on Architecture <term>Vastu: the science of sacred and domestic space</term> and the exploration of underground water <term>Dakargala: the art of dowsing or finding water sources</term> as stated in the Samhitas are of great importance. The characteristics of temples, the "Ayurveda of trees" <note>botany and plant health</note>, and the considerations for making divine idols are also well-described in this Samhita branch. Beyond these, the results of the cries of crows, the howling of jackals, and the gestures of cows, horses, and deer are investigated. The Samhita portion holds a primary position in the science of Jyotisha <note>astrology/astronomy</note>.

Garga, Parashara, Asita, Devala, Kashyapa, Bhrigu, Vashistha, Brihaspati, Bhadrabahu, and Varahamihira are the famous authors of the Samhita texts. Among them, the work "Brihat Samhita" by the teacher Varahamihira remains the most widely used and celebrated.

## Muhurta <note>Auspicious Timing</note>

The word "Muhurta" refers to a specific division of time. Even in the Vedic period, Muhurta is seen used as a synonym for a measure of time. A fifteenth part of the day is designated by the term Muhurta; the same applies to the night. Thus, in total, there are thirty Muhurtas in one single day <note>a full day-night cycle</note>. Authoritative statements on this are found in the Upanishads and Brahmanas. For example:

> "Now, what he says: Chitra, Ketu, Data, Pradata, Savita, Prasavita, Abhishasta, Anumanta. This is indeed that. These are the Muhurtas of the day. These are of the night."

->(From the Taittiriya Brahmana 3-10-6)<-

Different names for the Muhurtas are found in the Brahmanas and other texts, categorized by the bright and dark fortnights and the distinction between day and night. For example:

> "Chitra, Ketu, Prabhan, Abhan, Sambhan, Jyotishman, Tejashvan, Atapan, Tapan, Abhitapan, Rochana, Rochamana, Shobhana, Shobhamana, Kalyana."

->(From the Taittiriya Brahmana 3-10-1)<-

<summary>This page outlines the diverse subjects covered in the Samhita branch of astrology, including meteorology, architecture, and omens, while defining the Muhurta as a specific unit of time based on Vedic scripture.</summary>
<keywords>Samhita, Muhurta, Varahamihira, Brihat Samhita, Vedic timekeeping, omens, Vastu, Dakargala, Taittiriya Brahmana</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of timekeeping, specifically focusing on the names of the Muhurtas (traditional units of time approximately 48 minutes long) as found in Vedic literature.</meta>

<page-num>vi</page-num>
# The Jewel of Auspicious Moments

These are the names of the *Muhurtas* <note>A Muhurta is a traditional Indian unit of time lasting 48 minutes; there are 30 in a full day-night cycle.</note> for the day during the bright fortnight <note>Shukla-paksha: the fifteen days of the waxing moon.</note>. Regarding the *Muhurtas* of the night during the bright fortnight:

> “The Giver, the Great Giver, Bliss, Joy, and Delight. The Entering, the Settling, and the Resting; the Peaceful and the Very Peaceful. The Arising, the Originating, the Manifesting, the Well-born, and the Existing.”

(Taittiriya Brahmana 3-10-1,2)

The names of the *Muhurtas* for the day during the dark fortnight <note>Krishna-paksha: the fifteen days of the waning moon.</note> are as follows:

> “The Impeller, the Producer, the Radiant, the Illuminating, and the Shining. The Flaming, the Blazing, the Heating, the Deeply Heating, and the Scorching. The Bright, the Resplendent, the Beautiful, the Shimmering, and the Lovely.”

(Taittiriya Brahmana 3-10-1-2)

The *Muhurtas* of the night during the dark fortnight:

> “The Commander, the Assenter, Bliss, Joy, and Delight. The Placing, the Seating, and the Abiding; the Settled. The Pervasive, the Mighty, the Powerful, the Auspicious, and the Being.”

(Taittiriya Brahmana 3-10-1-2)

The fact that within every *Muhurta* there are also fifteen "sub-moments" <term>Sukshma-muhurta: micro-units of time used for precise astrological calculations</term> is found in the Taittiriya Brahmana. For example:

> “Now, when one says: 'Now,' or 'At that time'—that is it. These indeed are the moments within the moments.”

(Taittiriya Brahmana 3-10-6-6)

The names of these sub-moments are also found specified in the sacred verses. For example:

> “Now, Then, At this moment, Fast, and Quick. Swift, A blink, A breath, Running, and Running past. Hasting, Speeding, Rapid, Faster, and Velocity.”

(Taittiriya Brahmana 3-10-1-4)

In the *Atharva-Jyotisha* <note>An ancient astronomical and astrological treatise associated with the Atharvaveda.</note> as well, the word *Muhurta* is found as a designation for time. Their names, as well as the placement of the *Muhurta* within the four limbs <note>Likely referring to the divisions of the day or the body of the personified Time.</note>, are seen to be conceptualized. For example:

<vocab>Muhurta (Auspicious moment), Taittiriya Brahmana (Vedic text), Shukla-paksha (Bright fortnight), Krishna-paksha (Dark fortnight), Atharva-Jyotisha (Vedic astrology text), Sukshma-muhurta (Sub-moment)</vocab>

<summary>This page catalogs the poetic and technical names for time divisions (Muhurtas) during the day and night of both lunar fortnights as recorded in the Taittiriya Brahmana. It also introduces the concept of "sub-moments" and cites the Atharva-Jyotisha as a source for the structural placement of these units.</summary>
<keywords>Muhurta, Vedic timekeeping, Taittiriya Brahmana, lunar cycles, sub-moments, Atharva-Jyotisha, Sanskrit horology</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the introduction to a treatise on Muhurta (electional astrology), establishing its scriptural authority and practical necessity.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>
# Introduction

> A wise man should perform his actions using four factors to ensure success:
> the lunar day, the lunar mansion, the half-day, and the auspicious moment, daily. <note>original: "tithi-nakshatra-karana-muhurta"</note>

In the second chapter, twenty-fifth section of the *Nirukta* <note>An ancient Vedic text focused on etymology and philology</note>, the names for the measurements of time known as *muhurta* <note>a period of approximately 48 minutes</note> and *kshana* <note>an instant</note> are specifically mentioned.

Similarly, in the *Grammar of Panini* and in the *Mahabharata*, this word *muhurta* is frequently seen mentioned as a standard measurement of time.

As time passed, the scope of the meaning of the word *muhurta* became greatly expanded. People in the world believe that specific life-cycle rituals, such as marriage, as well as various other practical daily activities, grant the desired results only when performed in accordance with a prescribed time. Gradually, the practice arose of calling that specific time "Muhurta" in which—based on the requirements of a particular task—the mutual combination of the weekday, lunar day, and lunar mansion results in the greatest increase of virtues and the minimum of faults. Initially, descriptions of auspicious moments suitable for various tasks are found in the *Samhita* <note>compendium</note> texts. Because of the vastness of the *Samhita* branch of knowledge and the great utility of these auspicious moments, *Muhurta* attained primary importance. Thus, scholars have conceived of *Muhurta* as a distinct branch within the science of Jyotish <note>Vedic astrology and astronomy</note>.

### The Purpose of Muhurta

Only an action performed in harmony with the direction, place, and time yields the true fruit, and not otherwise. It is a matter of universal experience that even when many resources are available and constant effort is applied, many actions still produce undesirable results. Even modern thinkers agree that living beings and objects in the world are influenced by the specific rays of the Sun, Moon, and other planets situated in the sky. Considering through a disciplined, yogic insight how and when the influence of these planets and stars falls upon a person, and what the result might be, the ancient sages—for the benefit of the world—discovered the determination of the proper time for performing each specific action through many types of research and great effort. For this very reason, humans desiring successful results perform their daily and occasional duties only at the times stated by experts in the science of Muhurta.

## The Importance of Muhurta in Daily Life

The significance of <term>Muhurtas</term> <note>auspicious time-windows selected for beginning an activity</note> is already well-established regarding specific <term>Samskaras</term> <note>rites of passage</note> such as <term>Garbhadhana</term> <note>the rite of conception</note> and others. However, Muhurtas also hold a prominent place in the daily life of human beings. It is a matter of common experience in this world that everyone, from the unlearned to the scholar, performs their daily tasks in accordance with the demands of time. 

A farmer proceeds with plowing, sowing seeds, and harvesting only by looking toward the designated time. Similarly, in transactions like buying and selling, in the construction or first entry into a house, and in the digging of wells and tanks, people begin their work by adhering to a "good Muhurta." Even in traveling or taking medicine, people seek the favorability of time. By this, it is clearly expressed just how much importance the Muhurta holds in ordinary daily affairs.

## Subjects Generally Presented in Muhurta Texts

As stated previously, Muhurta texts were composed specifically to determine auspicious and inauspicious times so that one might attain the desired fruits of every specific action. Generally, in these texts, the auspicious and inauspicious combinations arising from the mutual alignment of the <term>Tithi</term> <note>lunar day</note>, <term>Vara</term> <note>weekday</note>, <term>Nakshatra</term> <note>lunar mansion</note>, <term>Yoga</term>, and <term>Karana</term> are described, along with the presiding deities of these lunar days and elements.

The following topics are investigated in Muhurta texts:
* Sections on times to be avoided (<term>Tyajya</term>).
* Deliberations on the <term>Sankranti</term> <note>the sun's transit into a new zodiac sign</note> and the determination of merit-earning periods (<term>Punya-kala</term>).
* The auspicious or inauspicious results resulting from each specific transit.
* The movement of planets (<term>Graha-gochara</term>), the positions of planetary obstruction (<term>Vedha</term>), the strength of the Moon, and the consideration of the nine <term>Taras</term> <note>stars</note> starting from one's birth star.
* Substances to be worn or used to remedy planetary afflictions.

Furthermore, these texts present:
* Suitable Muhurtas for rites of passage like conception.
* The <term>Kutas</term> <note>points of agreement</note> for matching the bride and groom in marriage.
* The periods of "infancy" or "old age" (states of powerlessness) of Jupiter and Venus.
* Royal consecration, sections on travel, and the pacification (<term>Shanti</term>) of evil omens encountered during journeys.
* <term>Vastu-vichara</term> <note>architectural science</note>, descriptions of various vehicles like elephants, and the methods for constructing mansions.

In Muhurta texts, the specific timings for rituals vary according to different regions. This is because, in our country, a difference in customs is observed based on the locale. Due to the specific traditions of North Indians or South Indians, variations in Muhurta texts become clear. Even within the South, a certain distinctiveness is perceived in the Muhurta texts of Kerala.

<gloss>Muhurta: Auspicious time; Garbhadhana: Conception rite; Samskara: Sacrament; Tithi: Lunar day; Vara: Weekday; Nakshatra: Star/Lunar mansion; Sankranti: Solar transit; Graha-gochara: Planetary transit; Vastu-vichara: Architectural inquiry</gloss>

<keywords>Muhurta, electional astrology, daily life, agriculture, Samskaras, planetary transits, regional customs, Vastu, Indian calendar</keywords>

## The History of Muhurta Texts

A brief overview regarding the history of Muhurta <note>Muhurta refers to 'Electional Astrology,' the branch of Vedic astrology used to determine the most auspicious time to begin an activity</note> texts is presented here. As previously stated, in ancient times, Muhurta was considered a part of the *Samhita* <note>Samhita: the branch of astrology dealing with collective phenomena like omens, weather, and kingdom-wide events</note> category. When considering when Muhurta attained its status as an independent branch, the consensus is that it occurred only after the time of the teacher Varahamihira. After Varahamihira’s work, the *Brihat Samhita*, no other comprehensive *Samhita* text of that caliber is found. It is reasoned that from this point forward, Muhurta gained prominence among *Samhita* topics, and the tradition of composing dedicated Muhurta texts began. Among the available Muhurta works, the *Ratnakosha* by Lalla is considered by experts in the field to be the very first dedicated Muhurta text. Historians are of the opinion that Lalla flourished in the 6th century CE. Based on this view, they determine the starting period of Muhurta literature to be the 6th century CE. Over time, many books on the subject of Muhurta appeared. Even within these texts, highly practical and useful ideas found a place, leading to the beginning of a tradition of composing shorter and more concise manuals.

## Some Famous Muhurta Texts and Their Authors

As time passed, the popularity of Muhurta texts expanded greatly. In the literature of *Jyotisha* <note>Jyotisha: the traditional Indian system of astronomy and astrology</note>, many Muhurta works are available. Here, a brief introduction to some famous Muhurta texts is presented.

| Name of the Text | Author | Time of Composition (Shaka Era) <note>The Shaka Era begins in 78 CE; to find the Common Era (CE) year, add 78 to these dates</note> |
|-----------|------------|---------------------|
| Ratnakosha | Lalla | 560 |
| Ratnamala | Shripati | 961 |
| Rajamartanda | Bhoja | 964 |
| Vidvajjanavallabha | Bhoja | 964 |
| Adbhutasagara | Ballalasena | 1090 |
| Vyavaharapradipa | Padmanabha | 1082 |
| Jyotirvidabharanam | Kalidasa | 1164 |
| Vivahavrindavanam | Keshava | 1165 |
| Vivahapatala | Sharngadhara | 1400 |

# Introduction to the Author

The name of the author of this work, the *Muhurtaratna* <note>The Jewel of Auspicious Moments</note>, which is the subject of our research, is Govinda Bhatta. It is known that he flourished in the village named Talakulam in the region of Kerala. Although details regarding his lineage or clan are not found anywhere in the texts, based on the statements of the commentator Parameshwara, it is understood that he was born into a family of Kerala Brahmins. Parameshwara remembers this Govinda in his own work titled *Achara Sangraha* <note>Compendium of Customary Practice</note> as follows:

> "The teacher of my grandfather was a twice-born man <note>term: 'Agrajanma' refers to a Brahmin</note> named Govinda, famous upon this earth. The customs taught by him, which reached me through the line of teachers, are set forth here."

By this, it becomes clear that this Govinda was the teacher of Parameshwara’s grandfather. At the beginning of the commentary on the *Muhurtaratna*, Parameshwara’s words further clarify this matter:

> "By the venerable Govinda, whose heart was sprinkled with compassion, this jewel of auspicious moments was extracted from the milk-ocean of the scriptures on time. Upon that work, by the grandson of his disciple, the one named Parama..." <note>The text cuts off, referring to Parameshwara himself</note>

<summary>This page provides a chronological list of major Sanskrit astrological works and introduces Govinda Bhatta, a medieval scholar from Kerala, as the author of the Muhurtaratna.</summary>
<keywords>Govinda Bhatta, Kerala astrology, Muhurtaratna, Parameshwara, Sanskrit manuscripts, auspicious timing, Talakulam</keywords>

<header>Introduction</header>

This small portion is being explained by the grace of the Lord, exactly as it was heard from the tradition.

## Determination of the Author’s Date

Regarding the date of the author Govinda, no direct information is found anywhere in the works composed by him. Nevertheless, based on the date of Paramesvara <note>A famous 15th-century astronomer and mathematician from Kerala</note>, who authored a commentary on the *Muhurtaratna*, the approximate time of this Govinda can be established. Historians conclude that this Govinda generally lived between 1234–1295 CE.

## Other Works of the Author

There is a significant difference of opinion regarding the other works of this author, Govinda. Some are of the opinion that this Govinda composed the commentary titled *Dashadhyayi* <note>Literally "The Ten Chapters"</note> on the *Brihat Jataka* of the great teacher Varahamihira. Others disagree, claiming that the author of the *Dashadhyayi* was a different person. This *Dashadhyayi* text is highly celebrated in the regions of Kerala. In this book, there is a strong conviction that the intention expressed by Varahamihira in his pledge—"brief, in varied meters, and rich in meaning"—is clearly realized. The author of the *Prashna Marga* <note>A seminal 17th-century treatise on Horary astrology</note> praises the *Dashadhyayi* extensively in his first chapter, as follows:

> "An astrologer who specifically desires to predict results should exert great effort in studying the *Dashadhyayi* commentary on the *Hora* <note>The Brihat Jataka</note>. || 30 ||
> 
> If one has not specifically put effort into the *Dashadhyayi*, the declaration of results becomes difficult; such is the saying of the learned. || 31 ||
> 
> He who wishes to predict results without having seen the *Dashadhyayi* is like one who wishes to cross the ocean without a boat." || 32 ||

By this, it becomes clear how significant the *Dashadhyayi* text was in the land of Kerala.

It is also said that this Govinda composed a text on electional astrology <term>Muhurta: the selection of auspicious times for beginning an activity</term> called *Muhurtapadavi*. However, there is also a difference of opinion suggesting that the Govinda who wrote *Muhurtapadavi* was someone else.

<keywords>Govinda, chronology, Dashadhyayi, Varahamihira, Kerala astrology, Prashna Marga, Muhurtapadavi, Paramesvara</keywords>

<page-num>xii</page-num>
->The Jewel of Electional Astrology<-

In this work, *The Jewel of Electional Astrology* <note>original: "Muhurtaratna"</note>, this Govinda has not mentioned any of his other composed works anywhere. Therefore, apart from this, no information is available regarding any other texts written by him.

# The Scholarship of the Author

If this Govinda is indeed the same author as the one who wrote the *Dashadhyayi* <note>A famous commentary on the Brihat Jataka</note>, then that work itself clarifies his profound scholarship. Even by observing the *Muhurtaratna*, it is understood that he possessed great experience in the science of Jyotisha <note>Astronomy and Astrology</note>. Through his style of verse composition, his use of alliteration, and his skill in presenting subjects, it is clearly evident that he possessed excellent mastery over the Sanskrit language and was highly learned.

This Govinda was experienced in all three branches <note>skandhas: Siddhanta (astronomy), Samhita (omens), and Hora (horoscopy)</note> of Jyotisha. Although the *Muhurtaratna* is a text concerning *Muhurta* <note>Electional astrology; choosing auspicious times</note>, he has nevertheless properly described mathematical procedures in certain places for the sake of clear understanding. The method for calculating the intercalary month <note>Adhimasa; an extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year</note> and the differences of opinion therein are found well-described. In the *Muhurtaratna*, from the 132nd verse to the 138th verse of the first chapter, much discussion is conducted regarding the intercalary month. He states the following regarding the method of calculating the intercalary month via the *Ahargana* <note>The count of elapsed days since the beginning of an epoch</note>:

> "The true self rests upon the Vedas..." <note>original: "satyātmā vedaśāyīti..." This verse uses a mnemonic system called Katapayadi to encode astronomical numbers</note> thus, when divided by the days multiplied by three and diminished... just as by the wise, these are the ghatikas <note>units of time equal to 24 minutes</note> in a cycle of years. || 133 ||

Stating that the intercalary month should be calculated only from the true longitudes of the Sun and Moon, he presents the opinions of others:

> "In the scriptures, rituals are not prescribed by the 'Brahma-mean' <note>calculations based on the Paitamaha Siddhanta</note> by those who know the meaning; others say intercalary months and rituals are determined by the mean positions of the Sun and Moon, while the followers of Rama <note>likely referring to the school of Ramadaivajna</note> say otherwise." || 137 ||

Similarly, in the third chapter, starting from verse 319 with the phrase "Known by the people of the day," the rising times of the constellations starting from Ashvini are stated. Likewise, the method for calculating the length of a shadow in digits <note>angulas</note> at a given time for the purpose of knowing the auspicious moment is propounded in this book.

This Teacher Govinda was not only a knower of the scriptures but also well-acquainted with regional customs. While describing the compatibility of the "Kutas" <note>points of compatibility</note> for marriage, he demonstrates his familiarity with the specific traditions of the time by showing the opinions of the Pandyas and the opinions of the Cholas <note>referring to the kingdoms and cultural regions of Southern India</note>.

<summary>This page evaluates the scholarship of Govinda, the author of Muhurtaratna, highlighting his expertise in both the mathematical and regional customary aspects of astrology. It specifically notes his detailed treatment of intercalary months and his awareness of South Indian traditions like those of the Pandyas and Cholas.</summary>
<keywords>Govinda, Muhurtaratna, Jyotisha, Adhimasa, intercalary month, Ahargana, Pandya, Chola, marriage compatibility, Sanskrit scholarship</keywords>

<page-num>xiii</page-num>
->Introduction xiii<-

While discussing the suitability of various astrological combinations <note>original: "yogas"</note>, he has also mentioned the differing opinions of others in various places.

### The Generosity of the Author

In the chapter on the "Rules for the Astrologer" <note>original: "Samvatsara-sutra"</note> in Varahamihira’s *Great Compilation* <note>original: "Brihat Samhita"</note>, the qualities required of an astrologer are thoroughly described. These specific virtues shine brightly in this Govinda. We recognize his qualities, such as his truthfulness and lack of envy, simply by examining this *Jewel of Electional Astrology* <note>original: "Muhurtaratna"</note>. His noble character is clearly evident from the statement he made at the beginning of the book. For example:

> Though the lore of auspicious timing <note>original: "muhurta"</note> was hidden by great scholars like Suvarnachuta and others who have kissed the ocean of astrology, it is revealed here in its basic directions. (2)

In the commentary on the aforementioned verse, Paramesvara writes: "It is heard that previously, a certain eminent twice-born <note>term: "dvija" - a member of the three upper castes of Hindu society</note> composed a work on electional astrology titled *All-Attainment* <note>original: "Sarvasiddhi"</note>. That work was not accepted by some. Then, this Teacher Govinda, wishing for the welfare of that excellent twice-born man, made an effort to incorporate that *All-Attainment* into his own work." By this, Govinda’s appreciation for the merits of others and his foresight are well expressed.

### Introduction to the Commentator

The name of the commentator of the *Jewel of Electional Astrology* is Paramesvara. This commentator, Paramesvara, sometimes refers to his own name as "Ishvara preceded by Parama" <note>This is a wordplay on his name Paramesvara, which combines 'Parama' (Supreme) and 'Ishvara' (Lord)</note>. It is understood that he specified it this way for the sake of the poetic meter. In the commentary on the present *Jewel of Electional Astrology*, he says:

> In that work, a small portion is being explained by Parama-preceded-by-Ishvara, the grandson of his disciple, according to what has been heard.

And likewise, in the *Light on the Sphere* <note>original: "Goladipika"</note>:

> Thus the arrangement of the celestial sphere has been briefly stated by Parama-preceded-by-Ishvara; there is more to be said regarding the sphere for his young son.

Thus he indicates his own name. He was a Brahmin of the Bhrigu lineage <note>original: "gotra"</note> and a follower of the Ashvalayana school of ritual. The village of Alathur, situated on the banks of the Bharathappuzha River (called the Nila River in Sanskrit) in the South Malabar region of the land of Kerala, was graced by his birth. This village of Alathur is known in the Sanskrit language as...

<summary>This page highlights the noble character of the author Govinda, who preserved the work of a predecessor, and introduces the commentator Paramesvara, identifying his lineage and his home in Kerala.</summary>
<keywords>Govinda, Paramesvara, Muhurtaratna, Alathur, Kerala, Varahamihira, astrology, Bhrigu lineage, Nila River</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical sketch of the astronomer Paramesvara, focusing on his geographical location, lineage, and the specific dates of his astronomical compositions.</meta>

<page-num>xiv</page-num>
<header>The Jewel of Electional Astrology</header>

The place is commonly referred to as *Vatasreni* <note>Sanskrit: "Row of Banyan Trees," known in Malayalam as Alattur</note>. This detail is mentioned by him in many of his works.
"Residing to the north of the confluence of the blue ocean <note>The Arabian Sea</note>," he states regarding his own country while composing his own commentary on the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>The "Sun Treatise," a foundational text of Indian astronomy</note>.

At the conclusion of his commentary on the *Goladipika* <note>"Light on the Sphere"</note>:
> "In a village eighteen *yojanas* <note>A measure of distance, roughly 8-9 miles per unit</note> west of the Prime Meridian,
> at a latitude measured by six plus six <unclear>...</unclear>"

Paramesvara describes his homeland in detail, stating he lived in a place eighteen *yojanas* from the Prime Meridian <note>The meridian of Ujjain</note> at a northern latitude of 10° 47'.

Regarding Paramesvara's parents, no details are available. However, he mentions in many places that his teacher's name was Rudra. Similarly, during his commentary on the *Goladipika*, the scholar Nilakantha Somayaji proclaims:

> "Paramesvara, having properly grasped the logic of mathematics and the celestial sphere even in his childhood from the masters of the sphere such as Rudra, Paramesvara’s son Narayana, Madhava, and others..."

Through this, details regarding Paramesvara's teacher are obtained. Paramesvara himself mentions during his commentary on the present text that his grandfather was a pupil of Govinda, the author of the *Muhurtaratna* <note>The Jewel of Electional Astrology</note>.

### The Date of Paramesvara

This Paramesvara himself mentions the time of his work's composition in his own *Drg-ganita* <note>"Computation in accordance with observation"</note>: "Thus, this *Drg-ganita* was composed in the Shaka year 1353 <note>1431 CE</note>." Furthermore, in the *Goladipika*:

He specifies the composition date as "In the Shaka year 1365 <note>1443 CE</note>." This Paramesvara, having reflected day and night for fifty-five years and having proven it through many observations, discovered the new system of *Drg-ganita* astronomy; Paramesvara himself states in his commentary on the *Mahabhaskariya* that this work reached completion in the year 1431 AD.

<summary>This page details the biography of the 15th-century astronomer Paramesvara, identifying his home in Alattur (Kerala), his lineage of teachers including Rudra and Madhava, and the specific dates he completed his major astronomical treatises.</summary>
<keywords>Paramesvara, Vatasreni, Alattur, Kerala school of astronomy, Drg-ganita, Nilakantha Somayaji, Madhava of Sangamagrama, 15th century, Indian mathematics</keywords>

<header>Introduction</header>

Based on these evidences, historians have determined that Paramesvara lived between 1365–1460 CE. <note>Paramesvara was a prominent mathematician-astronomer from the Kerala school, known for his long-term observations.</note>

**Other Works of the Commentator Paramesvara**

->This Paramesvara wrote many independent treatises as well as commentaries. His contribution is significant in both the theoretical and predictive branches of astronomy.<-

### Independent Works of Paramesvara:

1. *Compendium of Conduct* <note>original: "Acarasangraha"</note>
2. *Illumination of the Sphere* <note>original: "Goladipika"</note>
3. *Illumination on Computing the Planets* <note>original: "Grahanayanadipika"</note>
4. *Ornament of Eclipses* <note>original: "Grahanamandana"</note>
5. *Eight Verses on Eclipses* <note>original: "Grahanashtaka"</note>
6. *Computation of the Moon's Shadow* <note>original: "Chandrachhayaganita"</note>
7. *Method of Natal Horoscopy* <note>original: "Jatakapaddhati"</note>
8. *Computation based on Observation* <note>original: "Drigganita"; this was a landmark work that revised astronomical parameters to match observed reality.</note>
9. *Manual of Mnemonic Phrases* <note>original: "Vakyakarana"</note>
10. *Compatibility for Marriage* <note>original: "Vivahanukulya"</note>
11. *Results of the Good Divisions* <note>original: "Sadvargaphala"</note>

### Commentaries by Paramesvara:

|----------|----------|----------|

| *Explanation* | *Illumination of the Sphere* | Paramesvara |

| *Light on Siddhanta* | *Commentary on the Great Book of Bhaskara* | Govindasvami |

| **Description** | **The Treatise of the Sun** <note>Surya-siddhanta: a foundational Sanskrit astronomical text</note> | **Unknown** |

| **Paramesvara's Work** | **The Brief Manual** <note>Laghumanasam: an astronomical handbook</note> | **Munjala** |

In addition to these, it is indicated by Paramesvara himself during his commentary on the *Great Treatise of Bhaskara* <note>Mahabhaskariya</note> that he also composed a commentary on the "Lamp of Auspicious Moments" <note>Muhurtadipika</note>.

## The Erudition of the Commentator

The scholarly depth of Paramesvara is clearly evident from the sheer number of works he composed. This Paramesvara conducted a thorough study of the three branches of Astrology/Astronomy <note>The three branches or 'skandhas' are Siddhanta (mathematical astronomy), Samhita (omens/mundane astrology), and Hora (predictive horoscopy)</note> from the very beginning, and he was also experienced in other sciences. He possessed extraordinary mastery in the science of mathematics <term>Ganita-shastra: the mathematical branch of Indian astronomy</term>. According to his own statements, it is understood that through fifty-five years of constant hard work and verified observations, he discovered the new "System of Observed Computation" <term>Drig-ganita: a system of astronomical calculation based on direct observation rather than traditional parameters</term>. He wrote commentaries on the foundational treatises of masters such as Aryabhata and Bhaskaracharya. Through the composition of the *Light of the Sphere* <note>Goladipika</note>, a work concerning the celestial sphere, Paramesvara’s knowledge of spherical astronomy is clearly revealed.

He also composed a work related to predictive astrology titled the *Compendium of Conduct* <note>Acarasangraha</note>. During his commentary on the present *Jewel of Auspicious Moments*, he skillfully demonstrates his scholarship by occasionally quoting the words of ancient sages such as Vasistha, Garga, Parasara, and Narada. In necessary places within the commentary of the *Jewel of Auspicious Moments*, Paramesvara illuminates the core intentions of the original author through detailed explanations.

## Characteristics of the Annotated "Jewel of Auspicious Moments"

The present book possesses certain unique characteristics compared to other works on auspicious timing <note>Muhurta</note>. Although this book concerns the selection of auspicious moments, many ideas from all three branches of the science are seen discussed here and there throughout the work. For the sake of clear understanding, descriptions of topics related to Horoscopy, Mathematical Astronomy, and Omens are provided in the required places.

<summary>This page details the extensive bibliography and scholarly credentials of the astronomer Paramesvara, highlighting his 55-year development of the Drig-ganita system and his mastery over the three branches of Jyotisha.</summary>
<keywords>Paramesvara, Muhurtaratnam, Drig-ganita, Indian Astronomy, Aryabhata, Bhaskaracharya, Spherical Astronomy, Auspicious Moments, Sanskrit Commentaries</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory analysis of the Muhurta Ratna, focusing on the technical contents of the first four chapters.</meta>

<page-num>xvii</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

->Introduction<-

In the first chapter, from verse 148 to verse 153, there is an exposition of the six-fold divisions <note>original: "shadvarga"; these are the six types of divisional charts used to determine planetary strength</note> starting with the Ascendant, and the lords of the zodiac signs starting with Aries are mentioned. In the second chapter, from verse 839 to verse 846, there is a discussion on the mutual aspects <note>the way planets "look at" or influence one another</note> of the planets, their root-trine signs <note>original: "mula-trikona"; specific signs where planets are particularly powerful</note>, the temporal strength <note>original: "kala-bala"</note> of planets, and a consideration of directional strength <note>original: "dig-bala"</note>. From verse 847 to verse 855, the system of eight-fold points <note>original: "ashtakavarga"; a method of calculating planetary strength based on their positions relative to other planets</note> is discussed. Thus, the introduction of topics belonging to the Horoscopy branch <note>original: "Hora-skandha"</note> is seen.

In the first chapter, from verse 131 to verse 138, the characteristics of the intercalary months <note>original: "samsarpa," "ahaspati," and "adhimasa"; these are various types of extra months added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year</note> are described. The method for calculating these intercalary months using the total count of elapsed days <note>original: "ahargana"</note>, as well as the differences of opinion on this matter, are well-described in both the original text and the commentary. In verse 64, beginning with the phrase "multiplied by the equinoctial shadow digits" <note>original: "palangula-hate"</note>, the method for calculating the length of the day using the equinoctial shadow <note>original: "palabha"</note> is given. In the third chapter, from verse 319 to verse 321, the rising-sentences <note>mnemonic phrases used to calculate the rising times of constellations</note> for Ashvini and other stars are provided. To determine the auspicious time <note>Muhurta</note>, the method for calculating the shadow-digits of that specific time is given, and in verse 330, another method for knowing the equinoctial shadow is specified. In this way, the procedures of the Mathematical Astronomy branch <note>original: "Siddhanta"</note> are also shown in several places.

To understand the ease of obtaining commodities, a discussion explaining the increase or decrease of goods based on the specific production of fruits and flowers is detailed in the third chapter. In the fourth chapter, topics belonging to the Samhita branch <note>the branch of astrology dealing with collective omens and natural phenomena</note> are also found in this *Muhurta Ratna*, such as the consideration of underground water, the determination of water using the "Moon-Protection Circle" <note>original: "Chandragupti-chakra"</note>, and the characteristics of men and women.

In the first chapter, special technical names for certain stars such as *Sthuna*, *Kantaka*, and *Kantaka-sthuna* are mentioned. Topics such as the acceptance of the eighth day of the dark fortnight for marriage are also seen in this very book. Regarding the compatibility for marriage, twenty "Kutas" <note>points of compatibility</note> are specified in this text. Generally, in other texts on auspicious timing, only eight Kutas are mentioned. The gender, bird-association, and pulse <note>original: "linga," "pakshi," and "nadi"</note> of the stars starting with Ashvini are described in detail in this work.

In the second chapter:

> If Venus is in an Upachaya house <note>the 3rd, 6th, 10th, or 11th house</note> from the Moon, and Jupiter is in an Upachaya house from Venus, these three combinations are of great strength, whether occurring individually or in combination. || 251 ||

<term>Introduction (Upodghata)</term>: A formal preface explaining the scope of the work.
<term>Six-fold divisions (Shadvarga)</term>: A system of dividing zodiac signs into smaller portions to refine astrological predictions.
<term>Root-trine (Mula-trikona)</term>: Specific zodiacal zones where a planet's influence is most potent, second only to its exaltation.
<term>Eight-fold points (Ashtakavarga)</term>: A mathematical system used to predict the strength of a house based on the positions of all seven planets and the Ascendant.
<term>Horoscopy (Hora-skandha)</term>: The branch of astrology dealing with individual birth charts.
<term>Intercalary month (Adhimasa)</term>: An extra month inserted into the lunar calendar to maintain alignment with the solar seasons.
<term>Elapsed days (Ahargana)</term>: The total number of days passed from a fixed epoch to a specific date.
<term>Equinoctial shadow (Palabha)</term>: The length of the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox.
<term>Muhurta Ratna</term>: "The Jewel of Auspicious Moments," the title of the manuscript.
<term>Twenty Kutas (Vimshati-kuta)</term>: A complex system of 20 points used to measure the compatibility between a bride and groom, expanding on the standard 8-point system.

<summary>This page outlines the diverse contents of the Muhurta Ratna, showing how it integrates mathematical astronomy, individual horoscopy, and general omens. It highlights the text's unique features, such as the expanded 20-point marriage compatibility system and methods for finding underground water.</summary>
<keywords>Muhurta Ratna, planetary strength, calendar calculation, marriage compatibility, agricultural omens, water divining, Indian astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory material for the Muhūrtaratnam, focusing on specific auspicious combinations and a detailed summary of the first chapter's contents.</meta>

<page-num>xviii</page-num>
<header>The Jewel of Electional Astrology</header>

## Thus, the Full-Pitcher Combination (Pūrṇakumbhayoga):

> When the Moon’s son <note>Mercury</note> is rising in the constellation Rohini, or when Jupiter is in the ascendant or the tenth house <note>the 'sky' of the chart</note>,
> while the malefic planets are absent from the fifth, eighth, and twelfth houses, and the Moon is in its "Full" phase;
> This combination is indeed called the "Full Pitcher." A house established under this influence
> remains full of life, prosperity, wealth, sons, and wives for a thousand years. || 410 ||

The specific auspicious combinations mentioned previously are not found in other texts on *Muhūrta* <term>Muhūrta: the selection of the most auspicious time to begin an action</term>. While explaining the timing for various rituals, the differing opinions of other authorities are also presented in this book. Here, the unique characteristics of this work are presented in brief.

## Subjects Presented in the Current Work

This current text, the *Muhūrtaratnam* (The Jewel of Electional Astrology), is divided into four chapters. A brief introduction to each chapter is presented here:

## Topics of the First Chapter

At the beginning of the chapter, a detailed description of specific "afflictions" <term>Doshas: astrological flaws or unfavorable planetary conditions</term> is found. These include the *Vaidhṛta* and *Lāṭa* conditions, solar and lunar eclipses, the "junction points" <note>Gaṇḍānta</note> of constellations and lunar days, and the specific hours to be avoided in constellations such as *Aśvinī*. It covers the seasons starting with Spring; combinations leading to death based on constellations and lunar days; and "burnt" combinations <note>Dagdhayogas</note> arising from the union of lunar days and weekdays, such as the "Wildfire" and "Thunderbolt" combinations.

The text then discusses the neutralizing of these afflictions through the aspects of Jupiter and Mercury; the auspiciousness of the Moon; the *Karaṇas* <note>half-lunar days</note> such as the Lion; the rising times of *Viṣṭi* <note>an inauspicious timing</note> and *Gulika*; the trees associated with *Viṣṭi* such as the Palāśa; and the method for calculating the length of the day using the *Palabhā* <note>the shadow of a gnomon at noon on the equinox</note>. It further details the four great faults—Meteors, Earthquakes, the Staff of Brahma, and the Banner—based on the Sun's position in the constellations, along with their results. Other flaws mentioned include Smoke, the Solar Halo, the Staff of Indra, and Comets; the "Single Spike" <note>Ekārgala</note> flaw; the method for drawing the "Date-Palm Chart" <note>Khārjūrikā-cakra</note> to identify it; the specific minutes to be avoided at the end of the *Vyāghāta* yoga; the six types of lunar afflictions; and the classification of constellations as "Blind" or "One-eyed."

Finally, it identifies constellations known as the Pillar, the Thorn, and the Thorn-Pillar based on the Sun's position; the "Half-Strike" hours; the period to avoid a constellation after an eclipse and when it becomes usable again; the "infancy" and "old age" <note>periods of weakness</note> of Jupiter and Venus and the differing opinions on this; and the characteristics of the intercalary months known as *Saṃsarpa* and *Ahaspati*.

<summary>This page defines the highly auspicious "Full-Pitcher" astrological combination and begins a detailed table of contents for the first chapter, focusing on various astrological flaws (Doshas) and time-keeping methods.</summary>
<keywords>Muhurtaratnam, Purnakumbhayoga, electional astrology, astrological flaws, Doshas, lunar days, constellations, planetary aspects, Indian timekeeping</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<page-num>xix</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

The method for calculating the intercalary month <note>original: "Adhimasa"; an extra month added to the lunar calendar to synchronize it with the solar year</note> and the differing opinions regarding it are presented here. This section also covers the classification of lunar days <note>original: "Tithis"</note> into categories such as "Nanda" <note>the first, sixth, and eleventh days of a lunar fortnight</note> and others, along with their auspicious or inauspicious nature for specific activities. Furthermore, it discusses the six-fold divisions of signs <note>original: "Shadvargas"</note> including the decanates <note>original: "Drekkana"</note>, their presiding lords, the determination of the descending node <note>original: "Ketu"</note>, the auspiciousness of individual planets for designated tasks, and the consideration of the purity of the seventh house <note>original: "Saptamashuddhi"</note> for couples.

Additionally, exceptions to astrological flaws <note>original: "Doshas"</note> are explained. Various auspicious and inauspicious combinations <note>original: "Yogas"</note> are described, such as those formed by the union of constellations <note>original: "Nakshatras"</note> and weekdays, including the Maitri, Mangala, Shishu (infant), Tarunaka (youthful), and Sthaviraka (elderly) combinations, as well as the Nara (human) and Ahi (serpent) combinations. The first chapter also thoroughly describes the lords of the days, months, and years; the method for calculating the "Moon’s Desire" <note>original: "Chandreccha"</note> and "Moon’s Time" <note>original: "Chandravela"</note>; the results of the thirty-six time-segments; and the twelve states of the Moon's transit and their respective fruits.

This chapter is specifically titled "The Subject of Flaws, Their Exceptions, and Merits."

### **Introduction to the Second Chapter**

The second chapter provides the auspicious and inauspicious results based on the specific time of a girl's first menstruation. It details the compatibility of the bride and groom for marriage <note>original: "Kuta-anukulya"</note>, the twenty different types of compatibility factors <note>original: "Kutas"</note>, and their descriptions. It discusses inquiries regarding marriage, the resulting fortunes for the bride and groom, and twelve auspicious combinations. The auspicious and inauspicious times for specific life-cycle ceremonies <note>original: "Samskaras"</note> within the marriage rites are stated. This second chapter also details the auspicious moments <note>original: "Muhurtas"</note> for performing the ceremony of sitting near the fire <note>original: "Upasamveshana"</note>, the rite of conception <note>original: "Nisheka"</note>, the rite for ensuring a male child <note>original: "Pumsavana"</note>, the parting of the hair <note>original: "Simanta"</note>, the offering to Vishnu <note>original: "Vishnubali"</note>, the regulations for the confinement room <note>original: "Sutikagriha"</note>, and the birth ceremonies <note>original: "Jatakarma"</note>.

Furthermore, it covers the "fifth-house affliction" <note>original: "Panchamarista"</note> and its effects, the auspicious moments for the naming ceremony <note>original: "Namakarana"</note>, the letters associated with signs starting from Aries <note>original: "Mesha"</note>, "killer" letters, and the groups of stars categorized as "destroyer," "nourisher," or "servant." It also includes the rites for ear-piercing <note>original: "Karnavedha"</note>, the first outing of the child <note>original: "Upanishkramana"</note>, the first feeding of milk, the first feeding of solid food <note>original: "Annaprashana"</note>, and the first tonsure <note>original: "Chaula"</note>. It notes the auspicious times for oil massage and brushing teeth, the initiation ceremony <note>original: "Upanayana"</note> and the required strength of Jupiter <note>original: "Gurubala"</note> for it, the constellations prescribed for initiation, and the ten extremely auspicious combinations for initiation. Finally, it covers the commencement of Vedic studies <note>original: "Upakarana"</note>, the acquisition of knowledge, the ritual of girding weapons, house construction, the auspicious combination called "The Full Pot" <note>original: "Purnakumbha"</note> for building, the auspicious time for roofing, the constellations and days to be avoided for entering a new house, the consecration rites <note>original: "Pratishtha"</note>, the section on travel <note>original: "Yatra"</note>, specific foods to eat to neutralize constellation flaws during travel, auspicious and inauspicious omens for travel, forty-two auspicious combinations for travel, the coronation of a king, methods for subjugation <note>original: "Vashya"</note>, and combinations for enmity.

<summary>This page introduces the first two chapters of a Sanskrit astrological treatise, outlining the calculation of time, lunar days, and the timing of essential life-cycle rituals (Samskaras) from birth to marriage and house construction.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Muhurta, Samskaras, marriage compatibility, lunar calendar, house construction, travel omens, Upanayana, Jatakarma</keywords>

# The Gem of Auspicious Moments

Methods for the expulsion of enemies — Methods regarding four-legged animals <note>Livestock and pets</note> — Auspicious planetary combinations for the construction of stables for elephants and horses — Rules for clothing — Rules for physicians — Auspicious and inauspicious weekdays, lunar days, and lunar mansions for taking medicine — Rules for ancestral rites and fasting — The five divisions of the day: Early morning, Forenoon, Midday, Afternoon, and Evening — Rules for days when study is prohibited — Rules for entering new land — Rules for agriculture — Auspicious moments for plowing the soil — Rules for sowing seeds — Seven auspicious combinations for sowing seeds — Rules for the storage of grain — Auspicious weekdays, lunar days, and lunar mansions for the expenditure <note>selling or using</note> of grain. The number of rays of the Sun and other planets — Rules for buying and selling — Suitable lunar mansions and weekdays for digging wells — Medicine for diseases such as leprosy — The time for a ritual bath after recovery from illness — Rules for applying eye-salve — Suitable lunar mansions, lunar days, and weekdays — The "Spear-Chart" <term>Shula-Chakra: A diagnostic astrological diagram</term>, and through it, knowledge of the alleviation of disease or death — Rules for the worship of the Mother Goddess — Rules for the ritual of "Ascension of the Sacred Thread" — Rules for exorcism and sorcery — Combinations for taking earth to destroy enemies, including twenty-two auspicious combinations — Subjugation of enemies — The eight-fold strength of weekdays, lunar mansions, etc. — The presiding deities of the *Karanas* <note>half-lunar days</note> — Deities of the lunar days — Deities of the lunar mansions starting from Ashvini — The classifications of lunar mansions such as "Movable" and others — Deities of the zodiac signs starting from Aries — Actions prescribed during specific lunar mansions, lunar days, weekdays, *Karanas*, and zodiac signs — Consideration of the mutual aspects of the Sun and other planets — Consideration of enemies and friends among planets — The signs of "Root-Trine" <term>Mula-Trikona: Specific potent positions for planets</term> — The "Eight-fold Division" <term>Ashtakavarga: A system of calculating planetary strengths</term> — The piercing points <note>obstructions</note> of the planets — Substances to be worn to remove planetary defects — Sacrificial wood, priestly fees, and mantras for the ritual of pacifying the planets.

These considerations are detailed in the **Second Chapter**, which concludes with the rituals for the pacification of the planets.

## Introduction to the Third Chapter

In the Third Chapter, the results are first declared for two planets situated in the same degree of a single zodiac sign at the time of rising, and the results of Mars and others when situated in the divisions of the Sun and others. Furthermore: "Underworld" combinations — "Mountain" combinations — Actions to be performed during each weekday and its corresponding rising sign — The results of the rising of planets — Actions suitable to be performed during each lunar mansion and its corresponding rising sign — The "Joy" combination — The "Nectar" combination — The birds, genders, origins, and castes associated with the lunar mansions starting from Ashvini — The "voidness" <note>inefficacy</note> of lunar mansions in relation to specific lunar days and months — such considerations are described.

Additionally, where the equinoctial shadow <term>Vishuvat-Chhaya: The shadow of a gnomon at noon on the equinox</term> measures two inches, the "rising-sentences" <note>mnemonic verses for astronomical timing</note> for the lunar mansions starting with Shravana when they reach the meridian; the method for calculating the inches of the shadow at any given time to determine the auspicious moment; the rising-sentences for Ashvini and others <note>original: "uyavakyani" - likely a typo for "udayavakyani"</note>; the fifteen auspicious moments <term>Muhurtas</term> of the day and night and their names; the moments to be avoided based on the weekday; and the moments mentioned by the authors of the Puranas.

<keywords>Muhurta, Astrology, Agriculture, Medicine, Planetary Pacification, Lunar Mansions, Shadow Calculation, Rituals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory overview of the treatise, moving from the third chapter's miscellaneous topics to a detailed summary of the fourth chapter's contents regarding meteorology, dowsing, and architecture.</meta>

<page-num>xxi</page-num>
<header>Introduction</header>

By observing the state of flora <note>original: "vanaspatīnām"</note>, considerations such as the increase or decrease in the value of commodities and similar matters are examined in the third miscellaneous chapter.

### Introduction to the Fourth Chapter

At the beginning of the fourth chapter, various combinations for rainfall are described. This includes the possibility of rain based on the positions of the Sun and other planets in the zodiac signs; combinations for excessive rain <term>ativṛṣṭi: floods or torrential rain</term> and drought <term>anāvṛṣṭi: lack of rain</term>; the consideration of auspicious and inauspicious results arising from the conjunctions of Mars and other planets; and the characteristics of parhelia <note>original: "pratisūrya-lakṣaṇam"; optical phenomena like "mock suns"</note>.

Furthermore, it discusses the lack of rain or the results of heavy downpours based on the Sun's position in constellations <term>nakṣatras</term> such as Ashvini, or in various astrological divisions of time <term>karaṇas</term> like Kinstughna. It covers the knowledge of predicting rain through the "question-chart" <note>horary astrology or "Prashna"</note> and the investigation of groundwater. Various methods for detecting water are detailed, such as identifying the presence of water based on the location of grass, trees, or anthills on the ground.

Once it is generally known that water exists in the field desired by the inquirer, the method for constructing the "Moon-Secret Diagram" <note>original: "Candragupti-cakra"; a specialized astrological chart used for dowsing</note> is explained to pinpoint the exact location of the water. This includes the method of constructing the diagram according to the directions and different times of day, such as midday, as well as differing opinions among scholars on this matter.

The chapter also discusses:
* Determining groundwater based on the parts of the body touched by the inquirer.
* Identifying ancient, hidden wells.
* The characteristics of architecture <term>Vāstu-lakṣaṇa</term> and the description of sites suitable for habitation.
* Rituals of atonement <note>original: "śānti"</note> to remove defects in a site.
* The characteristics of the "Spirit of the Site" <term>Vāstu-puruṣa</term> and the layout of buildings.
* The auspicious and inauspicious results of constructing houses in specific sections of the land.
* The eight "Aya" formulas <note>mathematical calculations for architectural proportions</note> beginning with "Dhvaja" (the Banner), and the superiority of these formulas based on the cardinal directions.

Additionally, the fourth chapter specifies the characteristics of men—detailing the features of body parts starting from the shanks—as well as the characteristics of women to be avoided, and the praise of virtuous women.

<summary>This page outlines the contents of the fourth chapter, focusing on meteorological predictions, dowsing techniques for finding groundwater, and the principles of Vastu Shastra (sacred architecture) and human physiognomy.</summary>
<keywords>meteorology, rainfall prediction, groundwater dowsing, Vastu Shastra, architecture, physiognomy, Candragupti-cakra, planetary conjunctions</keywords>

<meta>This page introduces the manuscript sources used for the edition of the Muhurtaratnam, establishing the scholarly foundation for the text.</meta>

<page-num>xxii</page-num>
<header>The Gem of Electional Astrology</header>

# Description of the Manuscripts

This work is based on three primary manuscripts. Two manuscripts were obtained from the Oriental Manuscripts Library in Trivandrum (The Curator's Office Library, Trivandrum), marked as No. 131 T and 144A, 144B. The first is designated as T₁. The third was brought from the Government Oriental Manuscripts Library in Madras (Chennai), marked as No. 3295 M.

Manuscript T has been accepted as the primary source <note>The "codex optimus" or base text used for this edition</note>.

In manuscript T₁, many corrections are visible. There is nothing particularly unique regarding its variant readings.

Manuscript M, however, possesses certain unique characteristics compared to the other manuscripts.

The distinctiveness of that manuscript is shown here briefly. In this manuscript, subjects mentioned in other copies are sometimes written using different wording. It is possible that the scribe <term>Lipikara: a professional copyist of manuscripts</term>, while writing, recalled verses from other texts and made such changes due to confusion or memory. From this, it is clear that this scribe was also a learned man. A few examples are presented here:

## Table of Examples

| Reading in T | Reading in M | Variant Number | Page Number |
|----------|----------|----------|----------|
| For the sake of rules regarding emergencies | This rule for emergencies is declared | 1 | 95 |
| In the stars of Aja, Indra, Arya, Arka, Mitra, and Vishnu, it is said by the Sage | In the stars of Aja, Indra, Arya, Arka, Mitra, and Hari, it is ordained | 3 | 100 |
| In the conjunction of the Hasta star and the fifth lunar day | When the fifth lunar day is joined with Hasta | 1 | 110 |
| Of the very subtle word 'Ghati' | The word 'Ghati' refers to the subtle | 4 | 144 |
| That which was recited | That which was said | 2 | 213 |

<note>The table compares the standard text (T) with the unique variations found in the Madras manuscript (M), showing how the scribe used synonyms or different grammatical structures while preserving the astrological meaning.</note>

<summary>This page describes the three Sanskrit manuscripts used to compile this edition of the Muhurtaratnam, noting the locations of the original documents in Trivandrum and Madras. It highlights the intellectual quality of the scribes, specifically noting where one copyist substituted synonymous phrasing.</summary>

-># Introduction<-
<note>original: "Upodghātaḥ"</note>

Many such examples are found.

## Determination of the Original Manuscript
<note>original: "Mūlamātṛkānirṇayaḥ"</note>

The manuscript marked **T**, brought from the library at Thiruvananthapuram <note>formerly known as Trivandrum</note>, has been accepted as the original source manuscript.

1. A minimal number of errors are observed in this version.
2. Due to the specific characteristics of the script present in the manuscript, it is clear that the letters written here belong to the ancient Malayalam language script.
3. This manuscript is preserved on palm leaves. <note>original: "Tāḍapatra"</note>

For these aforementioned reasons, it has been determined that manuscript **T** should be considered the primary source among those available.

## Description of the Manuscripts

### Manuscript T

The Curators Office Library, Trivandrum.
*The Jewel of Auspicious Moments* <note>original: "Muhūrtaratnam"</note> with Commentary. C.O.L. No. 131.

Substance: Palm leaf. Size: 16 ½" x 2". Pages: 155. 8 lines per page.
Script: Malayalam. Complete. Subject: Astrology <note>original: "Jyotiṣa"</note>. [55 letters per line].

Beginning:

> Salutations to the great Lord Ganesha. May there be no obstacles.
> I bow to the Lord who is half-woman <note>referring to Ardhanarishvara, the composite form of Shiva and Parvati</note>, who shines like reddish-gold, who is adorned by the glittering crescent moon, who is a treasure of knowledge and an abode of compassion.
> By the venerable Govinda, whose heart was sprinkled with mercy, this *Jewel of Auspicious Moments* was drawn forth from the milk-ocean of the scriptures on time.
> A small portion of that work is being explained by the grandson of his disciple, named Parameshvara, according to what has been heard <note>learned through tradition</note>.
