It establishes the duration of these astrological faults and cites authorities like Brihaspati and Madhava to clarify that eclipse-related impurities only apply after the event.</summary>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of auspicious timing (Muhurta) from the previous section, specifically focusing on how planetary alignments can neutralize or create "burnt" (inauspicious) conditions for rituals.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>
->The Jewel of Electional Astrology<-

### Through the conjunction of the Moon and Venus, the lunar mansions attain auspiciousness.
### When Jupiter and Venus are combust <note>invisible due to proximity to the sun</note>, or when they aspect each other, or when they are visible to people during the day, then one should abandon auspicious activities. || 125 ||

### The intercalary months known as Sansarpa and Amhaspati <note>types of "extra" or "lost" months in the lunar calendar</note> are condemned. These six flaws do not apply to rituals that are not prescribed for a specific year <note>i.e., daily or monthly rites</note>. || 126 ||

---

Regarding the Moon, etc.: By the word "Udu" <term>Udu: a Sanskrit term for a Nakshatra or lunar mansion</term>, the stars beginning with Ashvini are meant. The meaning is that a lunar mansion that is "burnt" <note>rendered inauspicious by certain planetary positions</note> becomes auspicious when joined by the Moon or Venus. Thus, it is said in the *Madhaviya*: "The Moon, moving between a burnt lunar mansion and the planet that burns it..." This means that when a conjunction occurs between the stars and the Moon, it becomes auspicious. 

However, in the *Barhaspatya* text, it is said that a lunar mansion corrupted by planets returns to its natural state after six months. This is examined here: "A mansion joined by the Sun, Mercury, or Venus is respectively called 'burnt,' 'blazing,' or 'smoking'; one should abandon these for auspicious rites." This is stated in many scriptures. In that context, if the word "mansion" is taken to mean a Zodiac Sign <term>Rashi: a 30-degree sign of the zodiac</term>, then even a sign joined by Jupiter would have to be abandoned. 

Furthermore, an exception for burnt signs is mentioned in the scriptures: "A house burnt by the Sun, Saturn, Jupiter, or Mars returns to its nature after a month, a year, half a year, or a quarter year respectively; likewise for those corrupted by Rahu <note>the north node of the moon</note>, portents, or combustion." What is the intention of the authors of the scriptures here? Some say: "Blazing" and "Smoking" apply only to the stars <note>Nakshatras</note>, but "Burnt" status applies to the Zodiac Signs as well. Others say that the mention of the Zodiac Sign in the exception rule is to establish this. Because a star is a component of a Zodiac Sign, the sign is mentioned. Therefore, even if a planet has left the sign, if the "burnt" star is still present, the "burnt" quality remains. In the phrase "Rahu and portents," the word Rahu refers to an eclipse. Regarding other flaws: "Jupiter and Venus," etc.—this refers to rituals prescribed for specific days or months, but not those spanning a year.

---

It clarifies the technical distinctions between "burnt" stars (Nakshatras) and "burnt" zodiac signs (Rashis) in electional astrology.</summary>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the astronomical and astrological discourse from the previous section, focusing on the combustion of planets (their proximity to the Sun) and the specific periods when Venus and Jupiter are considered "infant" or "aged," during which they are inauspicious for rituals.</meta>

Page 27
Chapter One

# Chapter One

The Moon, Mercury, Venus, Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn <note>original: "shashankatah..." listing the planets in order from the Moon</note> are respectively dear, gentle, rhythmic, worshipful, wealthy, and honorable. When these planets enter the interior degrees relative to the Sun, they are indeed considered to be "set" <note>term: "set" (astaga/maudhya) refers to planetary combustion, where a planet is too close to the Sun to be seen and loses its astrological strength</note>. || 127 ||

When Venus rises in the East, it is a "child" for three days. In the West, it is a "child" for ten days and "aged" for five days. In the East, it is said by sages such as Atri and Vashistha to be "aged" for a fortnight <note>15 days</note>. Jupiter, however, is "aged" and a "child" for a fortnight <insert>regardless of direction</insert>; both states are to be avoided for auspicious ceremonies. || 128 ||

Other wise men claim these periods of infancy and old age are half the duration mentioned above. Venus, when situated in the sign or division of Taurus <note>original: "go-shastho"</note>, removes the defects arising from the sight of one's teacher. || 129 ||

The term "daytime" used here refers to the middle period between the forenoon and the afternoon. The first and last three *muhurtas* <note>a unit of time equal to 48 minutes; three muhurtas equal 144 minutes or 6 ghatikas</note> are designated as forenoon and afternoon respectively. || 130 ||

---

The verses beginning with "Dear" describe the portions belonging to the planets starting from the Moon and ending with Saturn. When these planets move above or below these specific degrees relative to the Sun and enter the middle space, they reach the state of "setting" or combustion. The word "indeed" <note>original: "kila"</note> is used to indicate that this is a general rule of thumb. 

Regarding the periods of "infancy" <note>balya</note> and "old age" <note>vriddhata</note> for Venus and Jupiter: When Venus becomes visible in the Eastern direction after emerging from combustion, it is a "child" for three days. When visible in the Western direction after combustion, it is a "child" for ten days. In the West, before the start of combustion, it is "aged" for five days. In the East, before the day combustion begins, it is "aged" for a fortnight (fifteen days). This is the teaching of Atri, Vashistha, and other great sages. 

Jupiter, however, remains "aged" or a "child" for fifteen days <note>margin: some manuscripts say five days</note>; for Jupiter, there is no distinction based on the direction of the horizon. Both "aged" and "infant" Venus and Jupiter must be avoided in performing auspicious rites. "Removes the defects of the teacher's sight" means that Venus, in the state described, nullifies the astrological affliction known as the "mutual sight" of teachers. When the word "daytime" is used by people, it refers to the interval between the forenoon and afternoon. The first six *nadikas* <note>24-minute units; 6 nadikas = 2.4 hours</note> are the forenoon, and the final six *nadikas* are the afternoon.

---

<summary>This page defines the combustion of planets and the specific "infant" and "aged" periods for Venus and Jupiter, during which they are considered powerless or inauspicious for religious activities. It also provides precise time measurements for the divisions of the day.</summary>

### Now, the Inquiry into the Intercalary Month

> A solar month containing two New Moon transitions is proclaimed as a *Samsarpa*. However, one that is devoid of even a single transition is known as an *Amhaspati*. || 131 || <note>original: "Samsarpa" and "Amhaspati" are technical terms for specific types of anomalous months in the Vedic calendar.</note>
> 
> If two mean conjunctions of the Moon and Sun occur between two mean solar transits of the Sun, then that is an <term>Adhimasa</term> <note>An extra or "intercalary" month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar seasons.</note>. || 132 ||
> 
> Using the mnemonic "Satyatma Vedashayi" <note>A numerical code representing 1,555,200,000</note>, after multiplying the remainder by three and dividing...
> As the wise say, these become the minutes of the day in a "Stambhaka" year. || 133 ||
> 
> Thus say some astronomers; others have spoken differently. [1/2]

---

Regarding the *Samsarpa* and *Amhaspati* months, it is said: "containing two transitions." In a true solar month where two true New Moon endings occur, that month is a *Samsarpa*. <term>In the interval of play</term> <note>original: "krīḍyantarāle"</note>, there are two mean conjunctions of the Moon and Sun; this means a double mean union. Then an <term>Adhimasa</term> (intercalary month) occurs. This means the extra month is produced within that specific month. 

Now, he describes the <term>calculation of the intercalary month via the count of elapsed days</term>: "Satyatma..." etc. From the <term>total count of days in the Kali Yuga</term>, subtract the value "Satyatma Vedashayi," multiply the remainder by three, and divide by the value "Yathadhira." The quotient represents the intercalary months that have passed. From the remainder, multiplied by thirty and divided by the previous divisor, the days and further fractions are found. Whatever years remain in the fractional part should be divided by "Stambhaka," and the resulting minutes should be added to the current intercalary month's fraction. The month in which this intercalary calculation reaches completion is the intercalary month. 

This method is mentioned by those learned in the tradition as being applicable for a time slightly after the <term>Saka era</term> <note>The Indian national calendar epoch starting in 78 CE</note>. According to mathematical logic, it happens exactly so. Or it may be done thus: "Subtract 'Gathadanam Shatangam' from the day-count... when the remainder is divided by 'Pathidhara' in whichever month it leaves no remainder, and the mean Sun is established, that becomes the intercalary month which halts all auspicious deeds." <note>Intercalary months are traditionally considered inauspicious for major life rituals like weddings.</note>

| 3. T₁: "containing two transitions" | 7. T: "Kshevah" |

# Chapter One

->¹The lunar months, beginning with Chaitra <note>The first month of the Hindu lunar calendar, usually March-April</note>, are those which contain within them exactly one solar transition—the meeting of the sun, moving at its true speed, with each individual zodiac sign starting from Aries. In this system, a month that has no such solar transition within its belly is called an **intercalary month** <term>Adhimāsa: an extra lunar month added to keep the lunar year in sync with the solar seasons</term>. If a month contains a double solar transition—meaning two transitions are swallowed within it—that month is called an **Aṃhaspati** <note>Also known as a Kshaya Māsa or "lost month"</note>. If such a month occurs, the month preceding it is called a **Saṃsarpa**. These two, the Saṃsarpa and the Aṃhaspati, are considered limbs of the year and parts of the seasons, and they occur together only after a very long time. In such a case, the intercalary month occurs later. (134)<-

->These three types of months—Saṃsarpa, Aṃhaspati, and Adhimāsa—are established by the true positions of the sun and the moon. They are known as **Malimlucha** <term>Malimlucha: literally "impure" or "thieving" months, referring to their status as "soiled" by the lack of a proper solar transition</term>. During these months, one should not perform auspicious deeds or rituals such as the Jyotiṣṭoma sacrifice. (135)<-

Having shown the opinions of these astronomers, he now shows the opinions of others with the phrase "Starting from Aries." The meetings of the sun moving at its true speed in each sign starting from Aries are the "solar transitions." The lunar months that contain exactly one of these transitions are known as Chaitra and so on. This is what is meant: the meeting where the sun, at its true speed, enters Aries from Pisces—whichever lunar month that occurs in is called **Chaitra**. The month in which the entry into Taurus occurs is called **Vaishākha**. The same applies to the others <margin>T: others</margin>.

In this context, a lunar month in whose "belly" (middle) no solar transition is possible is called an **Adhimāsa** (intercalary month). Thus, the definition of an intercalary month is the "emptiness" of a solar transition between two true new moon ends.

However, a lunar month that "swallows a pair of transitions"—meaning two solar transitions occur within the span of a single lunar month—is called an **Aṃhaspati**. This is the definition of an Aṃhaspati.

Regarding the phrase "Saṃsarpa occurs if it is above an Aṃhaspati": if an Aṃhaspati is going to occur in a month near a previously identified intercalary month, that month is called **Saṃsarpa**. It is not called an Adhimāsa because its status as a leap month is "blocked" by the Saṃsarpa. These two, the Saṃsarpa and Aṃhaspati, are considered parts of the seasons <note>Unlike a regular leap month, they are integrated into the seasonal ritual calendar</note>. An ordinary Adhimāsa, however, is not considered a limb of a season.

"Occurring together after a long time": once a Saṃsarpa occurs, an Aṃhaspati inevitably follows. After they occur in a certain year, they only appear again after many years have passed. "The intercalary month is later": this means that when an Aṃhaspati exists, the intercalary month that was expected earlier actually occurs later, somewhere after the Aṃhaspati.

He cites another text here: "The three months..." The Saṃsarpa, Aṃhaspati, and Adhimāsa are established by the true positions of the sun and moon, meaning they are defined by the true ends of the new moon. They are called **Malimlucha**, a name meaning "soiled months." During these, one should not perform auspicious rites, nor should one perform rituals like the **Jyotiṣṭoma** <note>A major Vedic fire sacrifice</note>.

---

# The Jewel of Auspicious Moments
<note>original title: Muhurtaratnam</note>

Intercalary months <note>Adhimasa: an extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar seasons</note> calculated from the true positions of the Sun and Moon are highly censured for auspicious activities. Some authorities claim these months should be determined by mean motion <note>the average speed of planets rather than their actual observed positions</note>, but that is not correct; I shall explain the reason why. || 136 ||

In the sacred scriptures, those who know the inner meaning have never prescribed that rituals be performed based on the mean positions of the planets. Others, such as the followers of Rama, claim that intercalary months arise from the mean positions of the Sun and Moon and even allow rituals during them. || 137 ||

When the Sun transitions into a new zodiac sign <note>Sankranti</note> only after the new moon day <note>Sinivali: the day when the moon is invisible</note> has passed, that is known as an intercalary month. It is prohibited for all religious acts. || 138 ||

The author explains further: Intercalary months occur based solely on the true positions of the Sun and Moon. This means that if no true solar transition occurs between two true new moons, an intercalary month is formed. Some claim the intercalary month is based on mean motion. This is not proper, because in the astronomical treatises <term>Jyotishashastra: the science of light/astronomy</term>, the experts have not prescribed rituals based on mean planetary positions. The reference to "Sinivali" comes from another text. 

Here, Govindacharya <note>a noted astronomical authority</note> has presented two views. The author has treated both views with equality, though he challenges them. First, he showed the definition of the intercalary month via mean motion, and then showed a simplified method for identifying it. By using the term "Astronomers," he offers praise to those who hold that view; otherwise, he would have simply said "some people." The second view is presented in many ways. Indeed, most authors of the scriptures only present the single version of the intercalary month they favor in their own works, not a second one. However, here both are shown to demonstrate balance. 

To the one who argues against many teachers by saying "some say it is not correct to use mean motion," we say this: Vidyamadhava stated, "If there are two new moons within one solar month, it is a 'Samsarpa' <note>an intercalary month that does not prohibit all rituals</note>. If there are two full moons in the sign of Revati, some call it 'Anhaspati' <note>a 'lost' or suppressed month</note>. If two mean solar transitions occur between two mean lunar conjunctions, that is an intercalary month. These three types of months are condemned." Some also say a Samsarpa occurs with two full moons. Even Suryadeva <note>likely the commentator on the Aryabhatiya</note> stated, "Between two mean solar transitions..."

---

|---|---|
| 1. Manuscript T₁: "Rituals are highly censurable" | 5. Manuscript T₁: "Astronomers" is written twice |
| 2. Manuscript T₁: "Rituals" | 6. Manuscript T₁: "Said to be good" instead of "Correct" |
| 3. This line is missing in Manuscript M. | 7. Manuscript T₁: "Should it be" (variant spelling) |
| 4. Manuscript T₁: "Mean planetary positions" | 8. Manuscript T: "By two full moons" |

It cites various authorities like Govindacharya and Vidyamadhava to reconcile different astronomical traditions.</summary>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>Chapter One</header>

## Chapter One

"If the conjunction of the **mean Sun and Moon** occurs [without a solar transit], it is an **intercalary month** <note>Adhimāsa: an extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar seasons</note>; if it occurs with the true positions, it is a **Sarpasarpas** <note>a specific type of intercalary month</note>, but not if it is an **Aṃhaspati** <note>a 'lost' or expunged month</note>." Thus, a commentator on the *Ashtaka* says: "If two mean new moons occur within the span of a mean solar transit, it is an intercalary month; however, if calculated by **true** positions, it is called an **Aṃhaspati** <gloss>Aṃhas</gloss> <note>original: 'Aṃhas'</note>, and is also known as **Anamas**." **Aṃhas** refers to the **Aṃhaspati** <note>the expunged month</note>. **Anamas** means <insert>free from</insert> **Amā** <note>the new moon day</note>.

Furthermore, our teacher states: "Thus, the value derived from the phrase **'King Kedāra'** <note>a mnemonic code representing a specific number in the Katapayadi system</note> represents the **mean Sun** starting from the **seconds of arc** <term>Viliptikā: a second of arc, the smallest standard division of a degree</term>. When the mean Sun resides in a particular zodiac sign, that is declared <gloss>declared</gloss> an **intercalary month**."

The meaning is as follows: The phrase **'King Kedāra'** provides the mean Sun's position in seconds. The phrase **'Born of the Earth'** <note>Dhijodbhava</note> represents the completed **revolutions** <term>Bhagaṇa: a full circle of the zodiac, or 360 degrees</term>. When the mean Sun is of this nature, an intercalary month occurs. The phrase **'Power, Atheism, Lord of Jinas'** <note>Prabhāvanāstikya-jinendra</note> also represents a value for the mean Sun in seconds. This value represents the time interval between two intercalary months. Therefore, by adding this to the mean Sun's position at the time of a previous intercalary month, one finds the mean Sun's position for the **second intercalary month**. By adding it again, one finds the position for the subsequent one.

Thus, the intercalary month is to be known through the **mean motion of the Sun**. As the ancient teachers said: "Two years, eight months, sixteen solar days, and thirty-eight **Ghatikas** <note>Nāḍī/Ghaṭikā: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> is the precise interval between intercalary months." Many teachers have proclaimed the intercalary month in this way.

"How can scholars assume this contradicts the scriptures? Only the **true** planet is fit for **ritual action** <note>Karma: religious ceremonies or astronomical applications</note>, not the **mean** planet." This <insert>logic</insert> <note>original: 'yukti'</note> which is commonly accepted does not apply to the intercalary month. The **mean planet** is taken as the basis for calculating the true position. Furthermore, no relationship is shown between two intercalary months [using true motion]. The famous sixty-year cycle starting with **Prabhava**, mentioned in the scriptures, is based on the **mean motion** of Jupiter. Since the scriptures define time through mean motion, there is no fault in this method. There is another reason for the two-fold definition, and it is neither solely mean nor solely true. One extra day arises every thirty-three days. An intercalary month is produced by **thirty** <gloss>those</gloss> such days. At the moment that intercalary month is completed, that month is called the **intercalary month**, just as the day of a solar transit is named.

# Now, the flaws of lunar days and other factors.

> A lunar day that touches two sunrises is called a "triple-touching" day <note>original: "trisprig"; this occurs when a tithi starts just before one sunrise and ends after the next</note>.
> A "lost" lunar day <gloss>1</gloss> is one that touches two [sunrises but is contained within them]; both are to be avoided in auspicious works. || 139 ||
> The lunar days are named Nanda, Bhadra, Jaya, Rikta, and Purna <gloss>2</gloss> in repeating order.
> Starting from the first day of the lunar fortnight, these are auspicious if the rising sign is "upward-facing" during a Rikta day. || 140 ||
> On the 4th, 6th, 8th, 9th, 12th, <gloss>3</gloss> and 14th lunar days, one should avoid
> the first 6, 6, 14, 25, and 10 ghatis <note>A ghati is a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> respectively <gloss>4</gloss>. || 141 ||
> Among the stars in the cardinal directions <gloss>5</gloss>, the middle one is the "Pillar"; <gloss>6</gloss> the three stars above it are the "Pot."
> The Pot where the Sun is located is "Empty," while the others and the Pillar are "Full." || 142 ||

---

"The flaw arising from time is discussed here, while the one arising from the region is mentioned elsewhere. The movement of the regional flaw is retrograde, while that of the time-based flaw is direct. Thus, the teachers have declared it to be of two kinds based on the distinction of time and region. Those twice-born <note>Brahmins</note> who become confused here due to hearing only a portion of the scriptures—for the removal of their confusion, this is explained here in detail." So it is said. Regarding "touching two sunrises": a lunar day that begins <gloss>7</gloss> before sunrise and ends after passing <gloss>8</gloss> the next sunrise is a "double-sunrise toucher." A lunar day that begins after a sunrise and ends before the next sunrise is called a "lost lunar day" <term>tithi-kshaya: a day that is skipped in the calendar</term>. "Upward-facing" refers to an upward-facing zodiac sign. "The 4th and others" <gloss>9</gloss> refers to the fourth, sixth, and subsequent lunar days. During these, the specified number of ghatis (6, 6, etc.) must be especially avoided at the beginning. <gloss>10</gloss> This is established by showing the exceptions to the prohibitions even for the sixth and twelfth days. Regarding "in the directions" <gloss>11</gloss>: The seven stars starting with Krittika are in the East. The seven starting with Magha are in the South. <gloss>12</gloss> The seven starting with Anuradha are in the West. The seven starting with Dhanishtha are in the North. Among these, the middle stars—Ardra, Hasta, Ashadha, and Bhadrapada—are designated as "Pillars." The group of three stars above the Pillar is designated as the "Pot." When the Sun is below the Pillar, <insert>A</insert> in all four directions, the three stars below the Pillar are designated as "Empty." The remaining stars are "Full." When the Sun is above the Pillar, in all four directions, the three stars above the Pillar are designated as "Empty." The remaining stars are "Full."

# Chapter One
Page 33

The rising of the stars <note>original: "nakshatra"</note> in their fullness destroys the defects associated with "empty" days <note>term: Rikta (empty days) — the 4th, 9th, and 14th days of a lunar fortnight, usually considered inauspicious</note>. For marriages, eating ceremonies, wearing ornaments, the tonsure <note>shaving of the head</note> of initiates, and the sowing of seeds, the Full Moon day <note>original: "purnika tithi"</note> is not to be condemned. || 143 ||

<insert>The term "Purnika" refers to the Full Moon day (Paurnamasi).</insert>

Some wise men praise the eighth day of the dark fortnight for marriage <note>original: "karagraha" — literally 'taking the hand'</note>. The strength of the planets and zodiac signs comes from their own inherent power, while the strength of the lunar days <note>tithis</note> and other factors depends on the strength of the Moon. || 144 ||

The results are classified as low, middle-low, medium, primary, and great fruit <note>maximum benefit</note>. The twenty positions starting from the first day of the bright fortnight are considered primary. || 145 ||

The five days following those are medium, and the five after those are low. The abundance of good or bad qualities in the divisions <note>vargas</note> is described in the verse concerning the elements. || 146 ||

The degrees related to the elements, tastes, and principles <note>referring to specific numerical degrees: 5th, 6th, and 25th</note> in their own trines <note>term: Trikona — a triangular relationship between signs 120 degrees apart</note> or in the signs of Aries and Cancer are considered. The "Pushkara" degrees <note>highly auspicious points in the zodiac</note> are condemned in some cases but praised when they align with the measures of the Samin, Manu, and Jina <note>referring to specific auspicious numerical counts</note>. || 147 ||

---

->Thus, in all four directions, "empty" stars and "full" stars can occur.<- Regarding the "Pillar" <note>Stambha</note>: When the Sun is situated in the "Pillar," that direction becomes "empty" or void. The upper and lower "jars" <note>symbolic divisions of time or space</note> are considered "full." "Purnika" means the Full Moon. Regarding "own strength": the planets, signs, and their divisions gain an excess of qualities through their own inherent power. The lunar days, ritual actions <note>karanas</note>, and constellations gain an excess of qualities from the strength of the Moon. Regarding "good and bad": in the verse beginning with "elements," it is explained that the Pushkara degrees for Aries, Taurus, Gemini, and Cancer—including their trines—are condemned if there is an abundance of "malefic divisions" <note>papavarga</note>. Conversely, for those same four signs, the Pushkara degrees are considered auspicious if there is an abundance of "benefic divisions" <note>shubhavarga</note>. "Principles" <note>tattvas</note> refers to twenty-five; thus, the twenty-fifth degree. "Taste" <note>rasa</note> refers to the sixth degree. "Jina" refers to twenty-four.

# Now, the Inquiry into the Sixfold Subdivisions and Related Matters

<sup>A</sup>The decan, the hour-half, the ninth-part, the thirty-part, and the twelve-part divisions are so named; whatever sign belongs to a planet, that is its "division." An "hour" refers both to the rising sign and to one half of a zodiac sign. <note>original: "Drekkana, Hora, Navamsha, Trimshamsha, Dwadashamsha." These are technical Sanskrit terms for specific mathematical divisions of a 30-degree zodiac sign.</note> || 148 ||

<sup>B</sup>Mars, Venus, Mercury, the Moon, the Sun, Mercury, Venus, and Mars; then Jupiter, Saturn, Saturn, and Jupiter—these are the lords of the signs and their portions. For the ninth-part divisions, the sequence begins from Aries, Capricorn, Libra, and Cancer respectively. The lords of the twelve-part divisions are determined sequentially starting from their own signs. <note>The text lists the planetary rulers of the zodiac signs in order from Aries to Pisces.</note> || 149 ||

<sup>C</sup>The degrees belonging to Mars, Saturn, Jupiter, Mercury, and Venus [in an odd sign] are measured by the numbers five, five, eight, seven, and five. In an even sign, these are reversed in both order and planetary lordship. The end of the signs Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces is known as the "junction of the constellations." <note>original: "Riksha-sandhi." This refers to the sensitive transition points between water and fire signs.</note> || 150 ||

---

Whichever subdivisions—such as the decans—belong to a specific planet, those are called that planet's "divisions" <term>vargas: the various harmonic divisions of the zodiac used to assess planetary dignity</term>. Among these, the "hour-half" <term>hora</term> belongs only to the Sun and the Moon. The "thirty-part" divisions <term>trimshamsha</term> belong only to the five planets starting with Mars <note>Mars, Saturn, Jupiter, Mercury, and Venus; the Sun and Moon do not rule these divisions.</note>. The "seven-part" division <term>saptamsha</term> is also a category. "Hora" is a name for the rising sign <term>lagna</term>, and "Hora" is also the name for one half of a sign.

Mars and the other planets are the lords of the signs starting with Aries, as well as the lords of the ninth-parts, twelve-parts, and seven-parts. In the signs Aries, Taurus, Gemini, and Cancer—along with their respective trines—the nine ninth-parts begin from Aries, Capricorn, Libra, and Cancer. The twelve-part divisions are equal portions of a sign, and their lords should be known sequentially starting from that specific sign. For example, in Aries, the twelve-parts begin with Aries, and their lords are Mars and so on.

In an odd sign, the degrees of the five planets (Mars, Saturn, Jupiter, Mercury, and Venus) are measured as five, five, eight, seven, and five. <margin>The text uses code-words for numbers: "Pavana" (5), "Samirana" (5), "Kaurpi" (8), "Juka" (7), and "Leya" (5).</margin> Here, numerical values are expressed through the names of signs. In an even sign, these portions and planets are to be applied in reverse order. This means the degrees for Venus, Mercury, Jupiter, Saturn, and Mars are measured as five, seven, eight, five, and five respectively. The final portion of Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces is called the "junction of the constellations" <term>riksha-sandhi</term>. Here, "portion" refers specifically to the ninth-part <term>navamsha</term>.

<header>Chapter One</header>

<margin>A</margin> Mars, the Sun, Jupiter, Saturn, Venus, Mercury, Venus, and Saturn, along with Mercury. <note>original: "Kṣitisuta-ravi-guru-saura-bhṛgu-budha-bhārgava-śanaiścarāḥ sabudhāḥ"</note>
<gloss>1</gloss> The Moon, Mars, and Jupiter are declared to be the lords of the <term>Decans</term> <note>A Decan (drekkāṇa) is a 10-degree division of a zodiac sign.</note>. || 151 ||
<gloss>2</gloss> In an odd-numbered sign, the <term>Hora</term> <note>A 15-degree division of a sign.</note> lordship belongs to the Sun and then the Moon; in an even sign, it belongs to the Moon and then the Sun. The placement of the <term>Septamshas</term> <note>A one-seventh division of a sign (4° 17' 8").</note> <gloss>3</gloss> begins from Aries, Scorpio, and Gemini. || 152 ||
From Capricorn, Leo, Pisces, Libra, Taurus, Sagittarius, and Cancer;
<gloss>4</gloss> and from Aquarius and Virgo, eighty-four types are considered. || 153 ||

# On the Defects of Planets

The destroyer of the defect of a malefic division is a benefic planet situated in the <term>Ascendant</term> <note>Lagna: the rising sign.</note>, a <term>Cardinal house</term> <note>Kendra: houses 1, 4, 7, and 10.</note>, or a <term>Trine</term> <note>Trikona: houses 1, 5, and 9.</note>.
If they are placed in the body (1st), the setting (7th), longevity (8th), friends (4th), dharma (9th), <gloss>5</gloss> children (5th), wealth (2nd), loss (12th), or action (10th) houses,
<gloss>6</gloss> the malefics <insert>(defects)</insert> cause a <gloss>7</gloss> gradually decreasing defect if they are not favorably disposed. || 154 ||
If placed in the body (1st), the setting (7th), intellect (5th), friends (4th), dharma (9th), <gloss>8</gloss> children (5th), <insert>(gains)</insert> (11th), wealth (2nd), or siblings (3rd) houses,
the benefics <gloss>9</gloss> produce a gradually decreasing merit if they are favorably disposed. || 155 ||

<gloss>10</gloss> In the verse beginning with "Mars, Sun, etc.," the three planets mentioned in sequence are the lords of the Decans for Aries, Taurus, Gemini, and Cancer respectively. Likewise, <gloss>11</gloss> for their <margin>B</margin> respective trines. In an odd sign, the first Hora belongs to the Sun, the second to the Moon. In an even sign, the first Hora belongs to the Moon. Regarding the signs, it means the seven signs starting from the seventh. "Eighty-four types" refers to the number eighty-four. This refers to the Septamshas. "Destroyer of malefic divisions" means it removes the defect arising from malefic <term>Navamshas</term> <note>Ninth-part divisions.</note> or <term>Dvadasamshas</term> <note>Twelfth-part divisions.</note>. <gloss>12</gloss> Malefics situated in the <insert>intellect</insert>, setting, etc., produce a defect that decreases in order. There is slightly less defect in the 7th than the 1st, slightly less in the 8th than the 7th, and so on. "If not favorably disposed" means this applies only if they are ill-placed; if they are well-placed, they produce only good. <gloss>13</gloss> Benefics situated in the mentioned places produce merit that decreases in order; this is if they are well-placed. If they are not well-placed, they produce only defects.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of astrological combinations (Yogas) used to determine auspicious timing (Muhurta), specifically focusing on how to neutralize the negative effects of malefic planets rising at the time of an event.</meta>

# The Jewel of Electional Astrology
## Page 36

If malefic planets are in the third, eleventh, or sixth houses, and two <term>benefics</term><note>Gentle planets like Jupiter, Venus, Mercury, or a waxing Moon</note> are aspected by each other while situated in the eleventh house or the <term>cardinal houses</term><note>The 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses, known as Kendras</note>; [156]
Or if a benefic is in a cardinal house from the rising sign or its <term>divisional ninth</term><note>Navamsha</note>, aspected by a friend and free from malefic influence;
Or if a strong Moon is aspected by benefics and no malefic is in the rising sign or its divisional ninth;
These three <term>combinations</term><note>Yogas</note> destroy the faults arising from a malefic planet rising in the ascendant. [157]

<term>Rahu</term><note>The North Node of the Moon, considered a shadow planet</note> gives its [negative] results for half a year or eighty days following an eclipse.
Even then, except for <insert>first feeding</insert> and marriage, it gives its results specifically when in its own <term>decan</term><note>A 10-degree subdivision of a zodiac sign</note>. [158]
Rahu in the ascendant will grant auspiciousness if a benefic planet is in a cardinal house.
All <term>invisible planets</term><note>Shadowy or secondary mathematical points in the sky</note> become powerless when the ascendant is strong. [159]
In whichever sign <term>Ketu</term><note>The South Node of the Moon</note> is seen in the sky in its various forms, it should be known to be in that sign; it is indeed the ninth planet. [160]
A strong Jupiter in a cardinal house destroys the "double-malefic" nature of the ascendant.
When in their signs of exaltation or their own auspicious divisions, and aspected by benefics and endowed with strength,
benefic planets are effective even in positions not specifically mentioned for auspicious rites. [161]

### Commentary
The three combinations mentioned in the half-verses starting with "If malefic planets are in the third..." are the destroyers of the faults of a malefic rising. Regarding the "results": Rahu gives particularly troublesome results for six months or eighty days starting from the time of an eclipse. The meaning is that after that, there is no fault. Even within that period, "except for first feeding and marriage": the meaning is that for a child's first solid food and for marriage, Rahu is always considered to give [negative] results during that half-year. In its own decan, it is always a fault. The statement "all invisible planets" refers to the many secondary points established by tradition. Regarding "In whichever sign": the movement, variety of forms, and time of appearance of the ninth planet, Ketu, are not [easily] obtained from the standard textbooks. When it occasionally appears on its own, it should be understood to be in the zodiac sign where it is seen at that moment. It should be known as the ninth planet. This is an exception to the general faults. Regarding "In exaltation": the meaning is that benefic planets will provide auspicious results [when so placed].

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of astrological timing (Muhurta), specifically focusing on when naturally "malefic" or unfavorable planetary risings can actually yield auspicious results based on their placement in specific houses.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
# Chapter One

->**The Auspicious Rising of Malefics**<-

When malefic planets are placed in the third, sixth, or eleventh houses, <note>These are the Upachaya or 'houses of increase' where malefics are considered helpful.</note> or when two benefics are aspected by benefics and situated in the angles <term>Kendras: the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses</term> or the eleventh house, then the rising of a malefic planet is auspicious. || 162 ||

When a benefic planet is situated in an angle from the degree of the rising sign <insert>Lagna</insert>, is aspected by its own or a friendly planet, and is placed in a favorable lunar mansion <term>Nakshatra</term>, then the rising of a malefic is auspicious. || 163 ||

When a benefic planet and the Moon aspect each other and are both strong, and if there is no cruel <note>malefic</note> planet in the degree of the rising sign, then the rising of a malefic is auspicious. || 164 ||

The aspect of Venus is auspicious for digging wells, irrigation, and new clothes. The Moon in the sixth or twelfth house is praised specifically for the wearing of new garments. || 165 ||

The lunar sign <insert>Chandra Lagna</insert> is highly honored for tonsure <note>shaving of the head</note>, travel, eating new grain, storing grain, and for the first hair-cutting and irrigation for women. || 166 ||

When the Moon is full in the rising sign, it is not forbidden for the first feeding of a child or for planting trees, provided malefics are in the third, sixth, or eleventh houses and benefics are in the angles. || 167 ||

***

**Commentary:**
Regarding the verse "Three, six...": When malefics are in the third, sixth, and eleventh—meaning those other than a malefic actually in the rising sign—and two benefics are aspected by benefics and located in the angles or the eleventh house, then the rising of a malefic becomes auspicious. In the phrase "or two benefics," the word "or" <note>original: "va"</note> should be understood as "and." Thus, in the *Treatise of Brihaspati* <note>An ancient authority on law and astrology</note> it is said: "When two benefics are in an angle or the eleventh, aspected by benefics, and malefics are in the third, sixth, or eleventh, then even a malefic rising is good." In some readings, the word "conjunction" should be applied.

Regarding "Rising sign...": This refers to being in an angle from the specific degree of the rising sign. It means staying in the center relative to the degree present in the rising sign. In the *Treatise of Brihaspati*, it says "situated in an angle from the degree of the rising." Regarding "placed in a favorable lunar mansion": the meaning is explained thus—the "sharp" and "dreadful" mansions are considered malefic. The remaining are auspicious. Alternatively, those suitable for a specific task are auspicious for that task, and others are inauspicious. As stated in the exception for "Blind Stars": "A strong benefic in an angle, rising with its aspect, destroys the defect of being born blind." <note>original: "upti"</note> means sowing or planting.

***

| 3. | T₁: "Situated in the angle of the rising degree" | 9. | T₁: "Should read" (variant spelling) |

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of auspicious timing (Muhurta), specifically focusing on planetary positions that neutralize defects and those that must be avoided during life-cycle rituals (Samskaras).</meta>

# The Gem of Auspicious Moments
<page-num>38</page-num>

->**The Gem of Auspicious Moments**<-

When planets are in their own signs or signs of exaltation <note>The highest point of a planet's influence</note>, they favor good deeds. Even if the Moon is in its own sign, Aries, or its exaltation, it destroys the defects of the ascendant <note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the time of birth or an event</note>, as does the Moon when placed in its own rising star-sign <note>original: "vaksyamanabhodaya"</note> which will be described later. **(168)**

In marriage, the pregnancy fire-ritual <note>Garbhahoma</note>, tonsure <note>ritual first haircut</note>, the first feeding of solid food <insert>Prasha</insert>, and the royal consecration <note>Abhisheka</note>, as well as the naming ceremony, one should avoid these houses <note>Zodiac signs/positions</note> if they are occupied by planets: specifically the seventh house and those following. **(169)**

The "fourfold set" consists of the ascendant and the Moon at birth, and the ascendant and the Moon at the time of the initiation ceremony <note>Upanayana</note>. A planet in the eighth house from these should be examined. Before the eighth year of age, this applies to all four; thereafter, it applies specifically to the middle two <note>the birth Moon and the initiation ascendant</note>. **(170)**

Likewise, Jupiter in the twelfth house is forbidden for the "binding of the weapon" <note>a warrior ritual</note>, but it is praised for the ritual to favor the birth of a male child <note>Pumsavana</note>. For tonsure and initiation, Venus and the Moon <note>original: "visita" - the bright ones</note> are auspicious for travel and eating. **(171)**

Venus and Mercury <note>original: "shashija" - son of the Moon</note> are also inauspicious for marriage and house-building. All other planets, when placed in the eighth house, cause grief during auspicious rites. **(172)**

---

The tradition-knowers avoid the full moon in the ascendant. "Aries" refers to the sign of the Ram. The Moon "destroys the defects of the ascendant" because it is the primary subject here. Regarding "the rising star-sign to be described": the specific ascendants for stars like Ashvini and others will be explained in the third chapter. This is called *Bhodaya*. The Moon situated there also removes the defects of the timing. "Consecration" refers to the royal consecration. "These houses" means the zodiac signs. "Occupied by planets" means they should be avoided if planets are present. He asks: which ones? The answer is "the seventh and following." Alternatively, the seventh and following houses are to be avoided when occupied by planets. Regarding "Birth": this refers to the fourfold set of the birth ascendant/Moon and the initiation ascendant/Moon. Before the eighth year, a planet in the eighth house from these must be scrutinized. This means a planet giving undesirable results there must be especially avoided. After the eighth year, a planet in the eighth house from the birth Moon and the initiation ascendant is specifically avoided. Likewise, for the "binding of the weapon," Jupiter in the twelfth house from the birth Moon and the ritual ascendant is avoided. For the male-child ritual, Jupiter in the twelfth is actually praised. In rituals like tonsure, the aforementioned planets in the eighth house cause "grief," meaning sorrow.

---

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of electional astrology (Muhurta), specifically focusing on the influence of the birth star (Janma Nakshatra) and the harmful "foot-strike" (Padaghata) aspects of planets during specific rituals.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>Chapter One</header>

# Chapter One

Leaving aside the first birth star, the two subsequent stars <note>the 10th and 19th stars from the birth star, known as the 'birth' group</note> are auspicious for the initiation ceremony, marriage, journeys, eating, and agricultural activities. || 173 ||

For a bride, all three birth stars are considered excellent; this rule applies specifically to the groom as previously stated. However, for the first bath after menstruation, the three birth stars are highly condemned for both men and women. || 174 ||

In the rites of shaving, the parting of the hair <term>Simanta: a traditional pregnancy ritual</term>, administering medicine, the first appearance of the menses, and the application of perfumes, the birth star is specifically rejected. || 175 ||

The Sun destroys the day <note>the star</note> that is its own; the Moon destroys the seventh from the full-moon star; Mars destroys the third; Jupiter the sixth; Venus the best <note>the eleventh</note>; || 176 ||

Saturn destroys the ninth; and Rahu destroys the fifth. These are the "foot-strikes" <term>Padaghata: a malefic planetary aspect</term> in sequence. Whatever affliction is caused by a planet, that affliction becomes fruitless <note>neutralized</note> if that planet is strong. || 177 ||

From Saturn, the twentieth star is called 'Ganda' and the seventh is called 'Kala' <note>Time/Death</note>. The Sun and other planets cause death when placed in the twelfth, the enemy house <note>6th</note>, the house of death <note>8th</note>, the ninth, or the tenth. || 178 ||

The Sun and others in the seventh or fifth, and Rahu in the twelfth, are givers of death. Whatever affliction is caused by a planet, if that planet is strong, the affliction is neutralized. || 179 ||

---

In auspicious works, all planets placed in the eighth house cause suffering. Regarding the "Bride": in marriage, are the second and third birth stars to be accepted while avoiding the first? As stated, this distinction applies only to the groom. For the bride, all three birth stars are auspicious. "Highly" means "excessively" in the context of the bath. "Appearance of the mat" refers to the first appearance of menses. "The Sun from its own star": all planets destroy the star at the number indicated by their own position. This means they spoil it. The Moon, when full, destroys the seventh star from the star occupied during the full moon. This flaw is known as "Foot-strike" (Padaghata). From the star occupied by Saturn, the Sun and others situated in the twelfth and other positions mentioned cause death. Rahu in the twelfth also causes death.

---

It introduces the concept of 'Padaghata' (planetary foot-strike), where planets spoil certain days unless the planet itself is transitionally strong.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of electional astrology (Muhurta), specifically focusing on the lunar positions and planetary influences that are considered auspicious or inauspicious for major life events like marriage and domestic rituals.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>The Jewel of Auspicious Moments</header>

## The Jewel of Auspicious Moments <note>original: "Muhurtaratnam"</note>

In all matters, one should avoid the eighth sign from the Ascendant <term>Lagna; the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the time of an event</term>.
Below thirty years of age, this is extremely harmful; below sixty, the effect is moderate. || 180 ||
Beyond that age, the learned say there is no fault.
The Moon situated in the eighth division <term>Amsha; a sub-division of a zodiac sign</term> is not desired in any ritual acts.
It is praised when in a beneficial division, but never in a division ruled by Venus <note>original: "Bhrigu-amshaka"</note>. || 181 ||
Divisions ruled by malefic planets should be avoided, especially during the dark fortnight <term>Krishna-paksha; the waning phase of the moon</term>.
Divisions ruled by benefic planets are praised; in the bright fortnight, the Moon is auspicious. || 182 ||
Even the Moon in the division of Libra, if full, causes no harm.
But even if full, the Moon in the division of Taurus is not desired by the wise when it falls in the eighth house division. || 183 ||
When the Moon is in a beneficial division and is aspected by a benefic planet,
then, being strong, it casts off the fault of its own association with cruel planets. || 184 ||
From the Ascendant of the auspicious moment, or from the natal Moon of the couple, the seventh house
is praised by the wise as a place of restraint <note>meaning the house should be free of planets</note> in marriage, just as it is for the Moon. || 185 ||
The birth sign of the woman, or the rituals mentioned for the "Womb-Oblation" <note>Garbha-homa; a collection of domestic rites</note>,
shaving, naming ceremonies, ear-piercing, building a house, and agriculture [should be free of malefic influence]. || 186 ||

---

The phrase "the sign only" means one should avoid the sign itself, but not necessarily its sub-divisions or the Moon within them. "Situated in the eighth division" means the division belonging to the eighth sign.
The Moon is praised in a beneficial division. The Moon is not praised in the eighth division if it belongs to Venus. "Malefic divisions" is a general rule, specifically regarding the eighth division. In the bright fortnight, the Moon is called "benefic." In the eighth house division, this rule applies. "Just as it is for the Moon" means that at the time of the auspicious moment, restraint <note>freedom from planetary occupation</note> is praised.
Likewise, the birth sign of the woman should be free of planets to be auspicious. It is seen in the scriptures that marriage should not be performed when the Sun, Mars, Saturn, Jupiter, or Mercury are in the birth sign. Regarding "Womb-Oblation": this is an explanation of what was said previously. By the term "Womb-Oblation," the seven rites starting from shaving and ending with the gift of a cow are indicated. <margin>Note: The word for Venus is not seen here in the list of planets to avoid in the birth sign.</margin>

---

| | | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |

<summary>This page details specific astrological prohibitions for the eighth house and lunar divisions, emphasizing how age and the lunar fortnight mitigate or worsen these effects. It also lists domestic rituals, such as naming and ear-piercing, that require the birth sign to be free of planetary interference.</summary>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on auspicious timing (Muhurta), specifically transitioning from specific prohibitions to general exceptions that cancel out astrological flaws.</meta>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>Chapter One</header>

The binding of weapons, the first cutting of a child's hair <note>original: "godana"</note>, the first feeding of solid food to an infant, and the consecration <note>original: "abhisheka"</note> of kings and others for the sake of prosperity—these have been discussed. || 187 ||
The flaws <note>astrological afflictions or "doshas"</note> associated with these acts, as well as their specific exceptions, have been stated. Now, the general exceptions <note>original: "apavada," referring to planetary combinations that nullify bad omens</note> to those flaws are being recorded. || 188 ||

### Now, the Exceptions to the Flaws

If the Lord of the Ascendant <note>the planet ruling the rising sign</note> is placed in a favorable division, is strong, and is joined or aspected by benefic planets; and if there is a proper combination of the prescribed planet, lunar mansion <note>nakshatra</note>, lunar day <note>tithi</note>, and zodiac sign; || 189 ||
And if the Moon is placed in the aforementioned positions, situated in a benefic division <note>amsha</note>, and performing a good action; and if all benefic planets are situated in the signs or divisions of gain <note>the 11th house</note> relative to the sign occupied by a benefic; || 190 ||
If the Ascendant is as many signs away from the sign occupied by Jupiter as the Sun is from the Ascendant; if the Moon is "seated on a throne" <note>a specific potent position</note>, or "riding an elephant" <note>gajaga</note>, and is constantly worshiping the gods; || 191 ||
If the benefics are in odd signs from the Nanda division and the malefic planets are in even signs; and if any one among the Lord of the Ascendant, the Hour <note>hora</note>, the Year, the Month, or the Day is strong; || 192 ||
If the Ascendant and the Moon are aspected by the lords of the divisions they occupy and are endowed with strength; if Jupiter is in its sign of exaltation, in its own division, or in its own Ashtakavarga <note>a system of points used to calculate planetary strength</note> with high points. || 193 ||

---

In this context, "feeding" refers to the first solid food given to a child. "Consecration" refers to the sprinkling of holy water for the prosperity of kings and others. Thus, the flaws and their exceptions have been stated. Now, the general exceptions to the flaws are demonstrated. Regarding the phrase "Lord of the Ascendant": each half-verse describes one specific yoga <note>planetary combination</note>. In the phrase "seated on a throne," only one such state is possible at a time. Regarding "all benefics": "division of gain" means the division of the eleventh sign. Here, if one planet is in the eleventh sign/division from the sign/division occupied by a benefic, the yoga is formed even if the other planet is also a benefic. So it is said in the <term>Barhaspatya</term> <note>A classical text attributed to the sage Brihaspati</note>:
> "When a planet is situated in the eleventh sign or division from the sign or division occupied by Jupiter, the flaws perish just as the demons were destroyed by Rama."

Regarding "as many signs from Jupiter's division": "Jupiter's division" means the sign occupied by Jupiter's division. So it is said in the <term>Barhaspatya</term>:
> "As many signs as the Ascendant is from the sign of the division joined by the Teacher of the Gods (Jupiter), if the Sun is in that many signs, the flaws vanish in the assembly, just as an uneducated man vanishes in a gathering of scholars."

"High points" <note>original: "adhikarkshagah"</note> means situated in a sign with a high number of auspicious points in the Ashtakavarga system.

|---|---|
| 1. T$_1$ from the exception | 7. T$_1$ "consecration" for kings |
| 2. M situated in | 8. T$_1$ for the sake of prosperity |
| 3. T$_1$ dancing (for "constantly") | 9. T$_1$ are demonstrated |
| 4. T$_1$ Lord of the forest (for "Lord of the Day") | 10. T$_1$ seated on a throne |
| 5. T$_1$ own division | 11. T$_1$ they become |
| 6. T$_1$ in the clear division | 12. T "Just as one who has not studied" |

It cites the authority of Brihaspati to emphasize how a strong Jupiter or Sun can purify an auspicious moment.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Muhurta (electional astrology), specifically focusing on planetary strengths and auspicious combinations (yogas) that neutralize defects.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>The Jewel of Electional Astrology</header>

# If a benefic planet is in the sixteenth division <note>original: "shodasha"; referring to the Shodashamsha or 1/16th division of a sign</note> from the position of Jupiter, and the Moon and the Ascendant are in movable and fixed signs respectively,
# And if all planets are situated in the aforementioned places and are aspected by benefics, it is auspicious. || 194 ||
# A strong benefic situated in the 2nd, 7th, 12th, or 11th house from the Moon’s Navamsha <note>the 1/9th divisional chart</note> is favorable.
# Benefics in the 3rd, 11th, or 6th houses, or in their signs of exaltation or own divisions from the Ascendant or its Navamsha, are auspicious. || 195 ||
# If the lords of the Moon’s sign and its Navamsha are in the center <term>Kendra: the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses</term>, the trines <term>Trikona: the 1st, 5th, or 9th houses</term>, or the houses of increase <term>Upachaya: the 3rd, 6th, 10th, or 11th houses</term>;
# And if the lords of the Ascendant and its Navamsha are friendly to each other and aspect one another; || 196 ||
# Or if the lords of the Ascendant’s sign and its Navamsha are great friends and are aspected by Jupiter.
# With the Nanda <note>1st, 6th, or 11th lunar days</note> dates, and the stars Mula, Uttarashadha, Uttaraphalguni, Uttarabhadrapada, Pushya, Shravana, and Shatabhisha;
# With the Bhadra <note>2nd, 7th, or 12th lunar days</note> dates, and the stars Shravana, Dhanishtha, Shatabhisha, Purvabhadrapada, Uttarabhadrapada, Mula, Magha, Chitra, Vishakha, and Mrigashira; || 197-198 ||
# With the Jaya <note>3rd, 8th, or 13th lunar days</note> dates, and the stars Anuradha, Jyeshtha, Purvashadha, Uttarashadha, Shravana, Dhanishtha, Shatabhisha, Purvabhadrapada, Uttarabhadrapada, and Revati;
# With the Rikta <note>4th, 9th, or 14th lunar days</note> dates, and the stars Krittika, Rohini, Swati, Vishakha, Anuradha, Jyeshtha, and Shatabhisha;
# These combinations starting with Sunday <insert>and other days of the week</insert> destroy defects and increase merits. || 199-200 ||

---

Regarding "the Moon and Ascendant in movable and fixed": this means the Moon is in a movable sign and the Ascendant is in a fixed sign. Here, the "sixteenth part" is calculated according to the sequence of 108 divisions. These are general exceptions. Now, the auspicious combinations (Yogas) are described:
1. The nine stars starting from Mula, when combined with Nanda lunar dates and Sunday, form nine Yogas.
2. The ten stars starting from Shravana, when combined with Bhadra lunar dates and Monday, form ten Yogas.
3. The eleven stars starting from Uttara, when combined with Nanda lunar dates and Tuesday, form eleven Yogas.
<note>The text notes that Mrigashira, Chitra, and Dhanishtha are "half-stars".</note>
4. The nine stars starting from Anuradha, when combined with Jaya lunar dates and Wednesday, form nine Yogas.
5. The eight stars starting from Aryaman (Uttaraphalguni), when combined with Bhadra lunar dates and Friday, form eight Yogas.
6. The seven stars starting from Agni (Krittika), when combined with Rikta lunar dates and Saturday, form seven Yogas.
This means combinations occur with the seven groups mentioned alongside Sunday and the other days. These sixty-four Yogas destroy astrological defects and increase merit.

---

It emphasizes the relationship between the Ascendant, the Moon, and Jupiter in determining the fitness of a moment.</summary>

--- Page 81 ---
# Chapter One

The stars Vishakha, Anuradha, and Jyeshtha <note>original: "Vishvamitragnyah"; referring to the deities presiding over these stars</note> occurring on the second, fifth, or seventh lunar days when they fall on a Wednesday; the stars Anuradha, Jyeshtha, and Mula occurring on the thirteenth lunar day when it falls on a Thursday; || 201 ||

The stars Dhanishta, Shatabhisha, and Swati <note>original: "Vasuswatya"</note> or Rohini occurring on the *Bhadra* <note>The 2nd, 7th, and 12th lunar days</note> lunar days when they fall on a Saturday; the stars Shatabhisha, Dhanishta, and Shravana <note>original: "Shatavakhya-aryama-upeta"</note> occurring on the *Nanda* <note>The 1st, 6th, and 11th lunar days</note> lunar days when they fall on a Friday; these are known as the **Nectar Formations** <note>Sudha-yogas; auspicious times for beginning tasks</note>. || 202 ||

The **Day Formations** <note>Vara-yogas</note> occur when Chitra, Ashwini, Shatabhisha, or Krittika <note>original: "Anala"</note> fall on a Sunday; or when Mula, Revati, Ashlesha, or Swati fall on a Tuesday <insert>along with a Nanda lunar day</insert>. || 203 ||

An auspicious day combined with the second, fifth, or seventh lunar day and the star Vishakha is exceedingly beautiful. The stars Anuradha, Ashwini, Bharani, Rohini, Pushya, or Hasta <note>original: "Mitra-ashvi-dvyaja-pushya-arka"</note> combined with either *Nanda* or *Bhadra* lunar days are auspicious. || 204 ||

The stars Mula, Ashwini, Vishakha, Uttara Bhadrapada, Krittika, Magha, and Purva Phalguni; as well as Vishakha, Ardra, Mula, Shatabhisha, Rohini, and Purva Ashadha... <note>The text continues to list stars for specific calculations</note> || 205 ||

---

There are three combinations of the three stars starting with Vishakha with either the seventh or fifth lunar days on a Wednesday. There are three combinations of the three stars starting with Anuradha with the thirteenth lunar day on a Thursday. There are three combinations of the three stars starting with Dhanishta with the *Bhadra* days on a Saturday. There are three combinations of the three stars starting with Shatabhisha with the *Nanda* days on a Friday. Thus, there are twelve **Nectar Formations**. There are four combinations of the four stars starting with Chitra with a Sunday and a *Nanda* day. There are four combinations of the four stars starting with Mula with a Tuesday and a *Nanda* day. These eight are the **Excellent Formations** <note>Varayogas</note>. There are four **Auspicious Formations** from the combination of the second, fifth, seventh, or thirteenth lunar days with auspicious weekdays. There are six auspicious formations from the combination of the six stars starting with Anuradha with either *Nanda* or *Bhadra* days. There are thirteen auspicious formations occurring from the sequential combination of the thirteen stars from Mula to Purva Ashadha, with the thirteen lunar days from the first to the thirteenth, and the thirteen weekdays from Monday to Saturday <note>in a repeating cycle</note>. Likewise, in the *Barhaspatya* <note>The Treatise of Brihaspati</note> it is said: "Mula with a Monday, and Ashwini with a Tuesday." "Punarvasu and Vishakha with a Monday, and Ardra with a Tuesday." And also: "Combined with the lunar days starting from the first of the <insert>bright</insert> fortnight."

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of auspicious timing (Muhurta), specifically focusing on planetary hours and the lords of the year and month.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>
->The Jewel of Auspicious Moments<- <note>original: "Muhurta Ratnam"</note>

### The Yoga called "Infinite" bestows complete enjoyment; the Yoga called "Giver of Happiness" bestows the enjoyment of wealth.
### The "Vision of Heaven" is a Daily Yoga <note>Nitya Yoga: a specific astronomical calculation</note> which begins at a certain number of ghatikas <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> after sunrise. || 214 ||

### The planetary hour, the mid-day portion, and the solar portion belonging to a malefic planet <note>planets like Saturn or Mars that are traditionally considered unfavorable</note> should be avoided, as should their lords.
### The Lord of the Day <note>Varesha</note> rules the first hour of the day; for the night, the first hour is ruled by the fifth planet from the day-lord, and the second by the sixth from that. || 215 ||

### The lord of the day on which the first day of the bright fortnight of the month of Chaitra <note>March-April</note> begins at sunrise is the Lord of the Year.
### The lord of the day on which the first day of the bright fortnight of Vaishakha <note>April-May</note> and subsequent months begins at sunrise is the Lord of the Month. || 216 ||

### The lunar date <note>Tithi</note> starting from the first of the bright fortnight, multiplied by two and divided by the duration...
### ...adding one, the sequence of days starting from Sunday is determined. The Moon and Venus are considered the least favorable in this context. || 217 ||

---

He explains the auspicious Daily Yogas: "Complete," etc. On Sunday, when 17 ghatikas have passed, combined with 5 ghatikas, the Yoga occurs. The same applies to others. A twelfth part of the day is called a "planetary hour" <term>Kala-hora: an ancient system of dividing the day into 24 hours, each ruled by a planet</term>. The same applies to the night. Thus, there are twelve planetary hours in the day and twelve in the night. The lord of the first hour of the day is the Lord of the Day. The second is the lord of the sixth hour from that. The third is the sixth from that sixth. Others follow the same pattern. At night, however, the first hour is ruled by the fifth planet from the Day Lord. The second is the sixth from that. Others follow. The planetary hour of a malefic planet should be avoided. The lord of the day at sunrise on the first day of the bright fortnight of Chaitra is the Lord of the Year. In that same month, he is also the Lord of the Month. In Vaishakha and subsequent months, the lord of the day at sunrise on the first day of the bright fortnight is the Lord of the Month. "Adding one" means plus one. "Sequence of days starting from Sunday" refers to the group of days beginning with Sunday. The application of this is seen in many ways by many authorities. The names of these days are called "Auspicious Days" and so forth.

---

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of lunar astrology, specifically focusing on the calculation and effects of 'Velas' (time-segments) and 'Abhilashas' (lunar desires) based on the moon's position.</meta>

# Chapter One: Page 47

The Day of Fortune, the Day of Strife, the Day of Benediction, and <note>original: "Kalakanthi"</note> the Voice of Time;
The Day of Victory, the Day of Slaughter, and the time of the "Destroyed Reflection" <note>A specific inauspicious astrological moment</note> in the divisions of the signs and minutes. || 218 ||
When the "Desired Star" <note>Abhilasha-nakshatra</note> is a favorable star, then actions performed are auspicious.
The success or failure of actions is known through the good or bad nature of the *Velas* <term>Vela: a specific time-segment or 'tide' of planetary influence</term>. || 219 ||

### The Thirty-Six Effects of the Time-Segments:
Headache, great affection, divine worship, and sitting at ease;
Eye disease, pleasant work, sporting with women, and high fever; || 220 ||
Golden ornaments, weeping, consuming poison, and sexual union;
Abdominal disease, playing in water, laughter, and painting pictures; || 221 ||
Anger, dancing, eating ghee <insert>clarified butter</insert>, and sound sleep;
Music, toothache, and engaging in quarrel; || 222 ||
Madness, bathing, and lying down;
Hunger, listening to the Scriptures, informal gathering, and fighting; || 223 ||
Good deeds, sinful deeds, harsh deeds, and rejoicing.
Thus are told the fruits of the *Velas* and the Lunar Desires within the zodiac signs. || 224 ||

Now, he describes the calculation for the number of the Lunar Desire and the Lunar *Vela*: <margin>The commentator refers to a verse starting with "Kha-kha-ashta" which is missing from the main text.</margin>
Multiply the current position of the Moon converted into minutes by eight hundred <note>original: "kha-kha-ashta" - literally 'space-space-eight'</note>, divide it, and place the remainder in two places. Multiply one by three and divide by two hundred <note>original: "kha-kha-netra" - literally 'space-space-eyes'</note>. The result is the measure of the "Desire" in terms of zodiac signs. Again, multiply the other remaining Moon-value by nine and divide by two hundred. The result there is the measure of the *Vela* in terms of minutes or *Drekkana* <term>Drekkana: a decan, or a 10-degree division of a zodiac sign</term>.

This is what was meant by "in the divisions of signs and minutes." Here, "minute" refers to the *Drekkana*. When the "Desired Star" is a favorable star, the actions performed then are auspicious—or rather, they *should* be performed then. Likewise, through the good or bad nature of the *Velas*, one should understand the auspiciousness or inauspiciousness of actions performed at that time. He lists the thirty-six fruits of the *Velas* corresponding to the *Drekkanas*, starting with "Headache." "Pleasant work" is one; "informal gathering" is another, meaning <gloss>playing as one pleases</gloss>. Thus the fruits of the *Velas* are told. The "Lunar Desires" will be explained in the context of the lunar signs mentioned. Here, whichever *Vela* is obtained from the current Moon, that one should be accepted. It means the <gloss>currently active Vela</gloss>.

# The Jewel of Electional Astrology
<page-num>48</page-num>
->**The Jewel of Electional Astrology**<- <note>original: "Muhūrtaratnam"</note>

The first twelve <term>states</term><note>avasthā: specific conditions or "moods" of a planet that determine its strength</note> are associated with the first <term>decan</term><note>drekkāṇa: a 10-degree subdivision of a zodiac sign</note> starting from Aries. The next twelve are situated in the middle decan, and the final twelve are in the last third of the sign. || 225 ||

In all constellations, the twelve states are known to last for five <term>nadis</term><note>nāḍī: a measure of time equal to 24 minutes</note> each. The Moon is said to be: Wandering, Healthy, Dead, Born, Playing, Laughing, || 226 ||

Bathing, Eating, Sleeping, Angry, Feverish, and Happy. These conditions related to the Moon should be understood as healthy or effective for those performing actions. || 227 ||

<margin>The results of the Moon's actions</margin>
One who is fallen from their position, a heard-of paramour, a thief, one mounted on a great elephant, one seated on a throne, a great king, a slayer of enemies, a commander of an army <note>original: "daṇḍinī"</note>, and one possessed of virtues. || 228 ||

One who is breathless, one with a severed head, one with cut hands and feet, a prisoner, and one who is dead. A king, one reciting the <term>Vedas</term><note>sacred Hindu scriptures</note>, one who is healthy, a rememberer of righteousness, and a doer of good deeds. || 229 ||

One of noble birth, a possessor of treasure, one who has ascended the scales <note>original: "ūḍhatula": likely referring to a ritual of weighing oneself against gold for charity</note>, one intent on scholarly exposition, a destroyer of enemies, one suffering from fever, one who is defeated, one exiled from their country, a servant, and one whose wealth is destroyed. || 230 ||

***

Regarding the connection of the time-periods with the decans, he says: "The first twelve..." etc. This means that the fullness or deficiency of the results of a specific time should be known through the auspicious or inauspicious connection of the decan with the planets. "In all constellations with five nadis"—in every constellation, each state lasts for five <term>ghatikas</term><note>equivalent to nadis, 24-minute units</note>. Thus, there are twelve states. He states the results of these states: "Wandering..." etc. There is also a reading "Moving." The word "Moon" <note>original: "Śaśī"</note> is connected to every state here. These ten <insert>twelve</insert> states are established by the Moon and are "healthy" (effective) for those performing actions. This means the result belongs to the performers of the work. He describes the Moon's actions: "Fallen from position" means displaced. "Mounted on a great elephant" means riding a choice elephant. "Commander" means possessing an army. "Breathless" <note>original: "vyasu"</note> means lifeless. "Cut hands and feet" means the limbs are severed. "Rememberer of righteousness" means one who contemplates Dharma. "Ascended the scales" means one who has climbed onto the weighing scales. "Wealth destroyed" means one devoid of money.

***

# Chapter One

->**The Sixty Lunar Activities**<-

Sitting in the assembly hall; a king carrying his bride; one frightened by an elephant; a coward in battle; one hiding in fear; one who feeds the priests and the sacred fire. || 231 ||

One pained by hunger while eating; <gloss>1</gloss> wandering and eating; one eating human flesh; one wounded by harsh words; <gloss>2</gloss> one getting married; one playing with balls; one playing at dice; a king who is deeply distressed. || 232 ||

<gloss>3</gloss> One who sleeps; one served by enemies; one attended by friends; a practitioner of Yoga; one accompanied by his wife; <gloss>4</gloss> one eating delicious food; one drinking milk; one worshipping a deity; one who is healthy; one sitting comfortably. || 233 ||

These are the Lunar Activities, assigned five by five to the signs starting with Aries <note>The zodiac signs are divided into groups to determine which "activity" the Moon is performing at a given moment</note>. One should predict the results based on the qualities associated with these, or by the activity alone. || 234 ||

The 4th, 6th, 5th, <gloss>6</gloss> 10th, 5th, 4th, 4th, 1st, 2nd, 2nd, 2nd, 4th, 1st, and 10th are considered inauspicious; the remaining Lunar Activities are auspicious in their respective order. || 235 ||

---

**Sitting in the assembly hall** means staying in the royal pavilion. **Carrying** means being with a wife. **Frightened by an elephant** means terrified of an elephant. **Hiding** means lost or destroyed. **Playing at dice** refers to gambling. **Attended by friends** <gloss>7</gloss> means served by friendly kings. **Accompanied by his wife** means with a spouse. These sixty Lunar Activities <gloss>8</gloss> occur one by one for every *nadi* <note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> of a lunar mansion (Nakshatra). By assigning five activities to each sign starting with Aries, one should state the result of the current activity based on the influence of the planets on that sign, whether <gloss>9</gloss> auspicious or inauspicious. Or, one may simply state the result of the activity itself, as that is the primary factor. Now, the author describes in order which activities are inauspicious and which are <gloss>10</gloss> auspicious, starting with the phrase "The 4th..."

---

|----------|----------|

| 2. Manuscript M reads "by marriages" | 7. T reads "seen" for "attended" |

<summary>This page details the final set of the sixty "Lunar Activities" (Chandra-kriyas), which are symbolic states used in Vedic astrology to determine the favorability of a specific moment. It explains how these activities are distributed across the zodiac signs and which specific sequences are considered unlucky.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of electional astrology (Muhurta), specifically focusing on the calculation of planetary strengths and the "qualities" (gunas) derived from the Moon's position and the rising sign.</meta>

# The Gem of Auspicious Moments
## Page 50

> <gloss>A</gloss> From the constellation where the Moon resides to the sign being considered,
> And from the Ascendant <note>Lagna</note> to the corresponding sign;
> One obtains the qualities in the same way within the Ascendant,
> <gloss>1</gloss> By the power of the <gloss>2</gloss> rising ghatis <note>A ghati is a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> in successive order. || 236 ||
> <gloss>B</gloss> "Those qualities" refers to the virtues arising from the favorable or unfavorable combinations of the signs.
> In all constellations, the first five ghatis are declared to be the sign of Aries. || 237 ||
> Again, the next <gloss>3</gloss> five ghatis shall be Taurus; one should continue to deduce the rest in this manner.
> Thus ends the section on Flaws, their Exceptions, and Qualities in the *Gem of Auspicious Moments*.

-> Chapter One <-

Now, he explains the determination of the results of actions through another method, beginning with "From the constellation where the Moon resides." In every nakshatra <note>lunar mansion</note>, the first five-ghati period constitutes the sign of Aries. The next five-ghati period is Taurus. In this way, Gemini and the others follow in five-ghati increments. The sign thus established by the ghatis of the constellation is what is referred to here as "the sign where the Moon resides." From that sign where the Moon is positioned at that time, however many signs away the observed sign is, the same number of signs away from the Muhurta Ascendant <note>the chosen time for an event</note> will be the resulting sign. The performer of the action obtains the <gloss>4</gloss> recorded qualities, known as "Lunar Activities" <note>Chandra-kriya</note>, of that specific sign. 

Furthermore, it says "by the power of the rising ghatis in the Ascendant in successive order." This means that among the five qualities obtained, the current quality is to be known based on the elapsed ghatis of the rising sign. By the word "ghati" here, a segment of six degrees <note>bhaga-shatkham</note> is implied. This is because the equality of signs is determined by degrees, not strictly by time units like ghatis. 

An example is given: "If the eleventh ghati is active in the Pushya constellation and the Ascendant is the first portion of Pisces, the quality of the sign Aquarius applies here, which is described as 'Sleeping'." Thus, where it says "endowed with those qualities," <gloss>5</gloss> the term "those qualities" refers to the virtues born from the conjunctions of favorable and unfavorable planets in the signs. In this manner, the flaws and their exceptions have been <gloss>6</gloss> demonstrated. Among these, <gloss>7</gloss> those who know the tradition <note>Sampradaya-vidah</note> hold that even if great flaws are accompanied by exceptions, they should still not be accepted.

Thus ends the first chapter concerning Flaws, their Exceptions, and Qualities, in the Commentary on the *Gem of Auspicious Moments* composed by Paramesvara, the grandson of Govinda’s disciple.

| 3. Text M; T₁ reads "five signs" for "five" | 7. Manuscript T: "those who know the tradition" |

It emphasizes that major astrological flaws should be avoided even if technical exceptions exist.</summary>
